# State of Wildfires 2025–2026

## Abstract

Abstract. The global geography, behaviour, and impact of fires are shifting, as spatially varying human influences interact with a visibly changing climate. The State of Wildfires project systematically tracks global and regional fire activity, analyses the causes of prominent extreme wildfire events for three focal regions, and projects the likelihood of similar events under future climate scenarios. During the March 2025–February 2026 fire season, global burned area (BA) was 18 % below the 2002–2024 average and global fire carbon (C) emissions were also 20–31 % below average, making it one of the lowest years on record. Yet this globally low fire year still produced widespread regional impacts. Canada experienced a third consecutive extreme fire year, with national fire C emissions at nearly triple the average since 2002, 70,000 evacuations, and reported direct losses of USD 360 million. In the Midwestern Canadian Shield Forests, the epicentre of Canada’s anomalous season, we find that anthropogenic climate change has doubled the likelihood of extreme fire weather in this region and made the observed BA around 61 times more likely during July–August 2025, while BA extent in the region is 46 times greater. Many parts of Europe also experienced record or high-ranking BA and fire C emissions, including in Spain, Portugal, and the UK, and a record USD 4.11 billion of physical assets were exposed to fire across the continent. Direct fatalities occurred in Türkiye (17), Spain (8), Portugal (6), Cyprus (2), Greece (1) and France (1), and evacuations in Türkiye, Spain, and Greece totalled over 110,000. In the Northwest Iberian Peninsula, we find that extreme fire weather over August 2025 was around 3 times more likely due to climate change. Under a high-emissions scenario (SSP3-7.0), fires of the same return interval are likely to burn about 78 % more area by the end of the century, with some projections within the same scenario suggesting increases of around sevenfold. In Chile and northwest Argentina, January–February 2026 fires caused 23 fatalities, displaced 55,000 people and threatened a UNESCO World Heritage Site. In the Chilean Temperate Forests and Matorral, the epicentre, we find that BA was around 36 % larger due to anthropogenic climate change. Across all focal regions in this report, the conditions for extreme fire developed gradually through months of precipitation deficits, cumulative drying and, in the case of Northwest Iberia, antecedent fuel accumulation aided by preceding wet periods. Continued climate change is expected to intensify these hydroclimatic extremes, yet mitigation has already reduced projected end-of-century wildfire risk: under a high-end emissions pathway (SSP3-7.0), the extreme BA events in our focal regions are projected to be 4–23 % smaller than they would have been under a worst-case emissions scenario no longer considered credible (SSP5-8.5). Meeting the Paris targets (SSP1-2.6) would further reduce BA during these events by 5–36 % across our focal regions. By integrating expertise from across the wildfire community with state-of-the-art observations, data, and modelling, this annual report advances understanding of regional wildfire extremes and their drivers, providing evidence to inform policy, preparedness, mitigation, adaptation, and broader societal benefit. Seventeen new and updated datasets created in this report are available from the State of Wildfires Project's Zenodo community (https://zenodo.org/communities/stateofwildfiresproject/).

- 150 

- 151 **Short Summary** 

- 152 The third State of Wildfires report examines extreme wildfire events from 2025 to early 2026. 153 It analyses key regional events in Midwestern Canadian Shield Forests, Northwest Iberia, and 154 Chilean Temperate Forests and Matorral, assessing their impacts, drivers, and attributing 155 them to climate change. Climate change greatly increased the likelihood of these fires, and 156 without strong mitigation, such events will become more frequent.

# 157 **1. Introduction** 158 

159 **1.1. Background** 160 161 Year after year, we are witnessing extreme wildfires occurring on every inhabited continent. 162 Research is showing that while global total burned area (BA) and emissions are decreasing, 163 regional impacts are becoming more severe. Fires are moving into extratropical carbon-rich 164 forests and peatland areas (Jones et al., 2024a; Zheng et al., 2021), and extreme heat and 165 drought are driving abnormally intense fires across Mediterranean biomes (Ghasemiazma et 166 al., 2026). Climate change is driving fire weather and resultant extreme fires that would be 167 very unlikely in a world without human-induced climate change (Jones et al., 2024b; Kelley et 168 al., 2025). As human population and urban area expand, we also see increasing extreme fire 169 effects at the wildland-urban interface (Seydi et al., 2025). As a result, over each successive 170 year of the State of the Wildfire report, we are seeing a continuing trend of increased regional 171 anomalies and societal impacts. Fire regimes are not just changing geographically, but are 172 also demonstrating changing characteristics, with the most extreme wildfires becoming more 173 frequent, intense, costly and deadly (Cunningham et al., 2024). Together, these trends 174 underscore the need for a more regional understanding of extreme wildfire behaviour, 175 emissions, and impacts. 176 177 Global BA decline is dominated by a reduction in low-intensity fires across African savannahs 178 associated with landscape fragmentation and fire suppression (Andela et al., 2017; Jones et 179 al., 2022). However, BA alone provides only a limited understanding of the observed changes 180 in spatially heterogeneous and highly complex contemporary fire regimes, with compound 181 drivers and impacts on both local and global scales. The number of extreme fires in areas that 182 are not typically fire-adapted is increasing, and larger, more frequent, more intense fires in 183 fire-evolved regions mean ecosystems and communities are struggling to adapt to the speed 184 of change (Cunningham et al., 2024; Grau-Andrés et al., 2024). 185 186 An increasing body of evidence shows that extreme fire weather is more likely with 187 anthropogenic climate forcing (Abatzoglou et al., 2025; Turco et al., 2026; Jones et al., 2022; 188 Jones et al., 2024b), with the extent of extreme BA also amplified (Kelley et al., 2025). Globally, 189 the last 11 years have been the hottest on record (C3S/ECMWF and WMO, 2025), and the 190 Arctic is warming nearly 4 times faster than the global average (C3S/ECMWF and World 191 Meteorological Organization, 2025; Rantanen et al., 2022). Concurrently, wildfires are 192 becoming an emerging risk in the Arctic and subarctic (Overland et al., 2026), with Canada 193 experiencing three years of unprecedented wildfire emissions over 2023-2025. 194 195 Climate change also contributes to compound events, including longer and more intense 196 heatwaves and drought. Europe is the fastest-warming continent, with current warming since 197 pre-industrial of around 2.5°C (C3S/ECMWF and WMO, 2025), and is seeing compounding 198 fire weather spatially with multiple regions facing extreme conditions simultaneously (Torres199 Vázquez et al, 2025; Gauthier and Bevacqua, 2026; Yin et al., 2026). Extreme heat and 200 drought can lead to abnormally large fires and more complete combustion, increasing 201 emissions and transport of those emissions for individual fire events. BA and emissions in 202 Europe reached record levels in 2025, with the largest contributions from the Iberian Peninsula 203 (C3S/ECMWF and WMO, 2025). Longer fire seasons can reduce safe burning windows, 204 limiting the capacity of communities to prepare and mitigate ahead of the fire season (Taylor 205 et al., 2025). Larger fires are also more difficult to suppress and control, potentially leading to 206 more destruction and loss of life. Over 2025-2026 for example, severe heat and drought have 207 driven record-breaking fires across Chile, the Aegean and Europe, with Spain experiencing its 208 largest BA of over 3,500 km<sup>2</sup> since at least 2002 (Jones et al., 2026a). Across Greece, Türkiye, 209 and Cyprus, prolonged heatwaves drove destructive fires that displaced tens of thousands of

## 265 **1.2. Objectives of this Report** 

- 266 267 The State of Wildfires report brings together the latest science, tools and datasets for extreme 268 fire monitoring, attribution and modelling to track how wildfire impacts on society and the 269 environment are changing and to explain the drivers behind these shifts. It forms the 270 foundation of the wider State of Wildfires Project (2026; https://stateofwildfires.com/, last 271 access: 22 July 2026), which aims to deliver insights into climate, land-use, and fire 272 management policy to decision-makers and practitioners, ultimately supporting stronger 273 societal and environmental resilience to wildfire. In this edition of the State of Wildfires report, 274 we address the following objectives: 275 276 1. Identify extreme individual wildfires or extreme wildfire seasons globally over the period 277 March 2025-February 2026, and place them in the context of recent trends; 278 2. Shortlist three “focal” extreme events (extreme individual wildfires or extreme wildfire 279 seasons) with notable impacts on society or the environment, which are the subject of 280 dedicated analysis in subsequent report sections; 281 3. Assess the impacts of extreme wildfire events globally and for each focal event, in 282 terms of the exposure of population, physical assets (built environment), protected 283 areas and forest carbon projects to fire, as well as their impacts on air quality; 284 4. Diagnose the contributions of weather, fuel dryness, fuel load, ignitions, and 285 suppression to the occurrence of each focal event; 286 5. Attribute each focal event to anthropogenic influence by testing the role of climate 287 change on fire weather and BA extent; 288 6. Project future changes in the probability of each focal event under future climate 289 scenarios. 290 To address objectives 1 and 2, we build a comprehensive dataset of fire metrics, including 291 BA, fire counts, fire carbon emissions, and regional statistics of individual fire properties such 292 as size and rate of growth, all for consistent world regions, and quantitatively identify 293 anomalies in these metrics during the past fire season. For objective 3, we assess population, 294 assets, carbon mitigation projects and protected area exposure,  and conduct an air quality 295 impact assessment globally and regionally. We leverage available weather reanalysis, fuel 296 modelling and satellite observations and employ a state-of-the-art explainable AI fire modelling 297 framework (SPARKY, McNorton et al., 2024) to pinpoint the causes of the extreme fire events 298 of 2025-2026 for objective 4. To address objective 5, we calculate changes in fire weather 299 using an ensemble of models and observations in simulations with and without climate 300 change, and employ the probabilistic Bayesian model ConFLAME (Kelley et al., 2019; 301 Barbosa et al., 2025) to attribute changes in extreme BA extent. For objective 6, we project 302 future changes in fire season weather and the likelihood of the fire weather comparable to that 303 observed in 2025-2026. We use ConFLAME to project future BA under different climate 304 scenarios. We also analyse annual fire behaviour, covering BA, size, and intensity, using the 305 BURNN (Lampe et al., 2026) and Haas et al. (2026) models. 306 307 The State of Wildfires report was launched in 2024 and is an annual report that can harness 308 and adopt new methodologies developed by the scientific community in the interim between 309 its yearly publication. Over the coming years and decades, we aim to enhance the tools 310 presented in this report to predict extremes with increasing lead times, monitor emerging 311 situations in near-real time (NRT), and explain their causes rapidly, thus enhancing our ability 312 to deliver timely insights to decision-makers when they are most needed.

# 313 **2. Methods** 

### 314 

## 315 **2.1. Extreme Wildfire Events of 2025-26** 

- 316 

### 317 **2.1.1. Overall Approach and Definitions** 

- 318 319 As in our previous editions of this report, we define extremes in the 2025-2026 global fire 320 season using two overarching approaches: firstly, through assessment of Earth Observations 321 of fire from global satellite and data-driven modelling products (Section 2.1.1), and; secondly, 322 through consultation of panels of experts from six vegetated continents (Section 2.1.2). Since 323 our first report, the global fire season is defined as occurring in March-February windows 324 oriented around the annual minima of global fire activity in boreal spring (Jones et al., 2024b; 325 Boschetti and Roy, 2008; Giglio et al., 2013). This definition places the reporting boundary 326 after the austral fire season and before widespread burning in the boreal extratropics. 327 However, it is less well suited to northern South America, Southeast Asia, and Central 328 America. 329 

330 Due to the diversity of environmental settings in which fires occur and the range of ecological, 331 economic, or societal impacts caused, defining an extreme fire or an extreme fire season 332 remains inherently challenging and a universally accepted objective definition of “extreme” 333 remains elusive. To date, extreme fires have commonly been defined by their BA extent, 334 intensity, or severity, by their feedback on the global climate, and by their socio-economic and 335 ecological impacts (Linley et al., 2022, 2025; Driscoll et al., 2024; Cunningham et al, 2024; 336 Tedim and Leone, 2020). We reviewed the range of approaches that can be taken to identify 337 extreme wildfire events in our inaugural report (see Appendix B1.1 of Jones et al., 2024b). 338 Recognising these complexities and the need for transdisciplinary processes to establish 339 robust criteria in future work, this report maintains the deliberately broad and flexible definition 340 of extreme, precisely following the definition used in our previous reports (Jones et al., 2024b; 341 Kelley et al., 2025). For our full justification of this approach, please see Appendix B1.1 of 342 Jones et al. (2024b). 343 344 **2.1.2. Identifying Extreme Fire Seasons and Events from Earth Observations** 345 346 We catalogued the extreme regional wildfire events or annual fire seasons in the period March 347 2025-February 2026 based on anomalies in the distribution of several observable fire metrics 348 from Earth observations. Since our inaugural report on the 2023-2024 fire season (Jones et 349 al., 2024b), the observable metrics that we assess have included: regional fire counts; regional 350 BA and carbon emissions totals, and; regionally sampled statistics describing fire sizes and 351 rates of spread (95th percentile and maximum values). In our second report on the 2024-2025 352 fire season, we introduced one new metric: the average fire’s peak intensity calculated 353 regionally as the average of the 95th percentile fire radiative power (FRP) values observed for 354 individual fire perimeters (Kelley et al., 2025). In this report, we do not add further metrics but 355 we do: update the source of one carbon emissions dataset (GFED5 replaces GFED4.1s, see 356 Section 2.1.2.1); update the source of FRP observations (NASA FIRMS is now used, replacing 357 the MOD14A1 and MYD14A1 products, see Section 2.1.2.1); alter our calculation of the 358 carbon emissions anomaly, see Section 2.1.2.3), and; refine our calculation of the average 359 fire’s peak intensity (see Section 2.1.2.1). 360 361 All metrics are calculated for several regional layers shown in Table 1, which represent a 362 variety of biogeographical boundaries (e.g. biomes and ecoregions), geopolitical boundaries 363 (e.g. countries), and regions used in major scientific reports (e.g. by the Intergovernmental 364 Panel on Climate Change or the Global Carbon Project’s Regional Carbon Cycle Assessment 365 and Process). These layers are unchanged from our previous report.

### **_2.1.2.1. Input Data_** 

373 Table 2 lists all datasets, source variables, and derivative variables used in the current update 374 of the State of Wildfires Report. In brief, unchanged data sources include: BA is primarily 375 derived from the MODIS MCD64A1 BA product (Giglio et al., 2018); individual fire size and 376 rate of growth properties are derived from the Global Fire Atlas (Andela et al., 2019). Here, 377 we focus on describing the two changes made to input data sourcing since our previous report. 378 The reader is directed to our two previous reports for full details regarding other source 379 datasets (Jones et al., 2024b; Kelley et al., 2025), however a brief summary of the metrics 380 derived from each dataset is provided in Table 2. Note that all datasets and analyses were 381 updated through February 2026 to support our assessment of anomalous fire activity in the 382 2025-2026 fire season. 

- 383 

- 384 The first substantial change to our input data was the replacement of the Global Fire Emissions 385 Database (GFED) version 4.1s (GFED4.1s, van der Werf et al., 2017; as used in Kelley et al., 386 2025) carbon emissions with GFED5 (van der Werf et al., 2025; Chen et al., 2026a). GFED5 387 substantially revises GFED4s by using a larger, better-constrained BA estimate, though at a 388 relatively coarse resolution of 0.25° (Chen et al., 2023). It captures more small and agricultural 

- 389 fires than GFED4s and MCD64A1, and major updates from GFED4.1s include a 500 m fuel- 

8 

https://doi.org/10.5194/essd-2026-587 Preprint. Discussion started: 3 August 2026 _⃝_ c Author(s) 2026. CC BY 4.0 License. 





- 390 consumption model aggregated to 0.25° and updated emission factors (Binte Shahid et al., 391 2024), including dynamic savanna emission factors (Vernooij et al., 2023). GFED5 generally 392 gives higher carbon emissions than GFED4s, globally amounting to a 65% uplift in total 393 emitted carbon. However, the uplift is uneven, with especially large increases in fragmented 394 or human-dominated regions such as Southeast Asia, Europe and Central America, while 395 equatorial Asia decreases slightly because of recalibrated peat-fire emissions in GFED5 (van 396 der Werf et al., 2025). 

- 397 

- 398 The second significant change was the replacement of the Terra and Aqua Moderate 399 Resolution Imaging Spectroradiometer (MODIS) daily thermal anomaly products, MOD14A1 400 and MYD14A1, with the MCD14ML active-fire product, accessed through NASA Fire 401 Information for Resource Management System (FIRMS; Giglio et al., 2016, NASA FIRMS, 402 2026). MOD14A1 and MYD14A1 (used in Kelley et al., 2025) are gridded raster products 403 resampled to fixed 1 km spatial grids. By contrast, MCD14ML is a vector point dataset based 404 on the same underlying Terra and Aqua MODIS observations, but combining them into records 405 of individual fire-pixel detections with geographic coordinates, timestamps and FRP values. 406 This change impacts the 95th percentile of FRP values assessed for individual fires by 407 preserving the statistical distribution of individual detections, avoiding the distortion that occurs 408 when the pre-processed daily maximum values from MOD14A1 and MYD14A1 are used. 

- 409 

410 In our intercomparison of MODIS MCD64A1 with other available BA records, we now include 411 a comparison with the ESA MRBA60 BA product combining the FireCCI51 and FireCCIS311 412 BA products (Torres-Vázquez et al., 2026; Lizundia-Loiola et al., 2020; Lizundia-Loiola et al., 413 2022) for the period 2013-2026 (see Supplement S2.1), which replaces our comparison with 414 FireCCIS311 for the years since 2019 presented in Kelley et al. (2025). In addition, we 415 compare MODIS MCD64A1 observations with the VIIRS VNP64A1 BA product (Giglio et al., 416 2025) for the period 2013-2026 (see Supplement S2.1), extended from the years since 2019 417 in Kelley et al. (2025). 2013-2016 is the period of mutual observations by MCD64A1, VNP64A1 418 and FireCCI MRBA60.

### **_2.1.2.2. Input Data Uncertainties_** 

426 We do not substantially update our assessment of input data uncertainties in this report, as 427 our data sources are largely identical or highly related to those used previously. The reader is 428 referred to the full discussion provided by Kelley et al. (2025). Here, we highlight only the main 429 uncertainties and any effect of transitioning to new data product versions such as GFED5 and 430 MCD14ML (see Table 2). 

- 431 The MODIS MCD64A1 BA product is known to underestimate absolute BA, mainly because 432 small, short-lived, agricultural, or obscured fires are often missed at 500 m resolution. 433 Comparisons with higher-resolution data and products, including small-fire corrections 434 suggest under-detection of approximately 50%, and substantially higher global BA when small 435 fires are included (Chuvieco et al., 2022; Chen et al., 2023). However, these omissions are 436 relatively consistent through time, making MCD64A1 suitable for anomaly-based analyses of 437 regional variability and trends (Chen et al., 2023). 

438 Fire carbon emissions estimates are uncertain because of errors in BA or active fire detection, 439 fuel type and consumption, combustion completeness, and emission factors. GFED5 includes 440 improved BA corrections, 500 m fuel consumption modelling, and updated emission factors, 441 resulting in substantially higher global emissions than GFED4.1s and better agreement with 442 atmospheric constraints for some major fire events (van der Werf et al., 2025). However, 443 GFED5 uncertainties remain substantial and difficult to quantify, with cropland burning 444 highlighted as particularly uncertain because of limited field constraints on fuel consumption. 445 Global uncertainties for earlier GFED versions and GFAS are typically around ±20-25% at one 446 standard deviation, but new insights such as the importance of previously ignored small fires

### 504 **_2.1.2.4. Extreme or Anomalous Value Identification_** 

505 

506 As in our previous reports, we identified extreme regional fire seasons using the rank, 507 percentage anomaly, and standardised anomaly values of each observable metric outlined 508 above. Per Section 2.1, we intentionally define extreme events broadly, but for visualisation 509 purposes we highlight regions where 2025-2026 ranked among the top five fire seasons on 510 record, approximately corresponding to the upper quartile of the available observational 511 records from all fire seasons since 2002-03. 512 

513 We also characterised the timing of anomalous events on a monthly timescale by identifying 514 the month with the largest positive anomaly in BA, then defining onset and cessation as the 515 bounds of consecutive above-average months before and after this peak (constrained to 516 March 2025-February 2026). The resulting onset and cessation data for the 2025-26 fire 517 season are available from Jones et al. (2026b) and presented in Supplement S2.5. 518 519 **2.1.3. Identifying Extreme Fire Seasons and Events through Expert Consultation** 520 521 As in our previous reports, we assembled regional expert panels for each continent to provide 522 additional context on the extreme and impactful nature of the latest fire season (Table 3). The 523 panels bring vital knowledge of regional fire regimes, land management, exposure, and 524 vulnerability, making them well placed to judge whether specific fire seasons or events were 525 exceptional in context. They helped identify and describe events that were societally or 526 environmentally significant but may not have been fully captured by Earth observations 527 because of their scale, duration, timing, or obscuration by cloud or canopy cover. These 528 included fires associated with major suppression challenges, fatalities, evacuations, 529 displacement, infrastructure damage, air or water quality impacts, livelihood disruption, 530 cultural impacts, or disturbance to vulnerable ecosystems and ecosystem services. Rather 531 than applying fixed quantitative thresholds, we asked in-region experts to identify events 532 whose impacts were large enough to enter public or political discourse, drawing on regional 533 fire records, agency and disaster-management reports, news and social media. The panels 534 also contextualised fire seasons identified as extreme by Earth observations or regional 535 assessment, recognising that observed impacts reflect not only fire behaviour and climate 536 conditions but also exposure, vulnerability, governance, funding, and policy decisions. In this 537 way, the expert panels provide vital interpretation of which fire seasons and events should be 538 considered extreme, and why, beyond what can be inferred from Earth observations alone. 539 540 From this edition onwards, each continental expert panel publishes its regional assessment 541 as a standalone article rather than as an appendix to the core State of Wildfires report. This 542 change makes the panel assessments independently accessible and citable, gives 543 appropriate credit to the regional contributors who gather and interpret evidence for their 544 regions, and allows panels to involve additional experts whose experience is most relevant to 545 the latest fire season. It also helps keep the core report concise and accessible for a broad 546 readership. Section 3.3 therefore summarises the main findings of the continental 547 assessments, which are presented in full elsewhere (Dintwe et al., 2026; Kukavskaya et al., 548 2026; Fernandes et al., 2026; Kolden et al., 2026; Reynolds et al., 2026; Anderson et al., 549 2026).

## **2.2. Global Impact Assessments** 

556 **2.2. Global Impact Assessments** 557 558 In our report on the 2024-25 fire season, we introduced several global-scale impact 559 assessments including population, physical asset, and forest carbon project exposure to fire. 560 Analysis of the impact of fires on air quality was also introduced for selected regions. Key 561 advances in this report include: a refined method of accounting for population and asset 562 exposure through analysis at higher resolution; a new analysis of protected area exposure 563 extending from recent work by Resco de Dios et al. (2025); and the expansion of regional air 564 quality impact modelling to the global scale. Reporting of all analyses is now provided 565 consistently for the regional layers listed in Table 1, with anomalies for the 2025-26 fire season 566 also reported for all impact analyses. Anomalies are calculated and reported in the same way 567 as for Earth observations of fire (see Section 2.1.2.3). 568 569 **2.2.1. Population Exposure Assessment** 570 571 In the 2024-25 report, population exposure to fire was estimated using the CLIMate 572 ADAptation (CLIMADA) global risk assessment platform, following the gridded exposure 573 framework of Aznar-Siguan and Bresch (2019). In that approach, monthly MODIS MCD64A1 574 BA was aggregated to a 150 arcsec grid and multiplied by gridded population estimates from 575 WorldPop on a 4 km grid for the reference year 2018 (Eberenz et al., 2020), providing a 576 consistent global estimate of population exposed to BA at coarse resolution. For this report, 577 we replace that workflow with the high-resolution event-based method introduced by Teymoor 578 Seydi et al. (2025), which overlays annual WorldPop population data at ~100 m resolution with 579 individual fire perimeters from the Global Fire Atlas. This improves the spatial precision of 580 exposure estimates and allows exposure to be assigned to individual fire events. The new 581 approach also screens the Global Fire Atlas record to retain only wildland fires and remove 582 agricultural burns, urban heat signatures, persistent thermal anomalies, and hot-desert 583 artefacts that can spuriously inflate exposure estimates (see Teymoor Seydi et al., 2025). We 584 therefore define direct population exposure as the number of people residing within filtered 585 wildland-fire perimeters. Uncertainty propagates from the commission and omission errors in 586 the underlying BA products (Section 2.1.2.2). Misallocation of population in the WorldPop 587 density grid is most uncertain in cells with a high density of informal housing and less certain 588 in rural than urban areas (Thomson et al., 2022). Moreover, the metric only captures direct 589 exposure within fire perimeters and excludes indirect impacts such as smoke exposure or 590 precautionary evacuations (Teymoor Seydi et al., 2025). 591 592 The data produced using these methods are available from Teymoor Seydi et al. (2026). 593 594 **2.2.2. Physical Asset Exposure Assessment** 595 596 Physical asset exposure follows the same event-based fire-exposure framework as the 597 population exposure analysis, but replaces gridded population with gridded physical asset 598 value. In the 2024-25 report, asset exposure was estimated using CLIMADA (Aznar-Siguan 599 and Bresch, 2019) and the exposure layer LitPop for the fixed reference year 2018. LitPop 600 spatially distributes nationally produced capital estimates (World Bank, 2024) using night-light 601 intensity and population density (Eberenz et al., 2020). In the 2024-25 report, the gridded 602 asset values were multiplied by monthly MODIS BA fractions aggregated to a 4 km grid. For 

14 

https://doi.org/10.5194/essd-2026-587 Preprint. Discussion started: 3 August 2026 _⃝_ c Author(s) 2026. CC BY 4.0 License. 





- 603 this report, we retain the CLIMADA/LitPop approach for distributing produced-capital 604 estimates in space, but use an annual representation on a 1 km grid and intersect it with the 605 same filtered Global Fire Atlas wildland-fire perimeters used for the population exposure 606 analysis, following the event-based approach of Teymoor Seydi et al. (2025). This produces 607 fire-event-level estimates of the value of physical assets located within burned perimeters, 608 while reducing spurious exposure from agricultural burns, urban heat signatures, persistent 609 thermal anomalies, and hot-desert artefacts. As with population exposure, asset exposure 610 indicates the value of assets located within filtered wildland-fire perimeters and does not imply 611 damage or loss. 

- 612 

- 613 The data produced using these methods are available from Steinmann et al. (2026). 614 

- 615 **2.2.3. Air Quality Impact Assessment** 

- 616 As in our previous report (Kelley et al., 2025), we assessed the contribution of anomalous fire 617 activity to degraded air quality using the ECMWF Integrated Forecasting System configured 618 for atmospheric composition (IFS-COMPO) which is at the core of the Copernicus Atmosphere 619 Monitoring Service (CAMS) for the global domain. Fire smoke affects human health through 620 exposure to fine particulate matter (PM2.5), ozone, carbon monoxide and other toxic 621 compounds, with acute exposure linked to respiratory and cardiovascular illness, increased 622 mortality, reduced visibility, and wider mental-health impacts (Johnston et al., 2024; Xu et al., 623 2024). Seasonal biomass burning and fire emissions have impacts on local and transboundary 624 air quality in many regions around the world throughout the year (for example, in Southeast 625 Asia; Gu et al., 2024), but extreme fires can increase these impacts.  ECMWF IFS-COMPO 626 simulates trace gas and aerosol emissions, transport and removal at ~40 km resolution with 627 137 vertical levels. Daily fire emissions were prescribed from GFAS v1.4.2, which estimates 628 0.1° biomass burning emissions from satellite fire radiative power, with plume-rise injection 629 following GFAS/IS4FIRES (Kaiser et al., 2012; Rémy et al., 2017). 

- 630 In previous reports, air quality impacts were assessed only for selected focal events by 631 comparing simulations with and without local fire emissions. For this report, we instead ran 632 two global ECMWF IFS-COMPO simulations for the full 2025-2026 fire season: a factual 633 simulation using observed global fire emissions from GFAS, and a climatological simulation in 634 which 2025-26 fire emissions were replaced by a 2003-2023 fire emissions climatology from 635 GFAS (the climatological baseline). Both simulations include non-biomass-burning emission 636 sources of atmospheric pollutants described via CAMS pre-compiled inventories, or estimated 637 online when strongly dependent on meteorological conditions. The difference between the two 638 simulations isolates the contribution of anomalous fire activity during 2025-2026 to surface 639 PM2.5 concentrations. This global design allows fire-related air quality degradation to be 640 assessed consistently across all reporting regions listed in Table 1, rather than only within pre641 selected focal-event regions. 

- 642 Simulated surface PM2.5 concentrations were summarised for each reporting region as daily 643 population-weighted mean concentrations using 2020 population data from Gridded 644 Population of the World version 4 (Doxsey-Whitfield et al., 2015). We then compared factual 645 and climatological simulations to estimate the additional PM2.5 exposure attributable to 646 anomalous 2025-2026 fire activity compared to baseline exposure within each region. We 647 report the climatological baseline and observed number of days on which regional population648 weighted daily PM2.5 exceeded the World Health Organization 24-hour guideline of 15 µg m ⁻ ³ 649 (World Health Organization, 2025). 

- 650 The data produced using these methods are available from Di Tomaso et al. (2026). 651 

- 652 **2.2.4. Protected Area Exposure Assessment** 653 

15 

https://doi.org/10.5194/essd-2026-587 Preprint. Discussion started: 3 August 2026 _⃝_ c Author(s) 2026. CC BY 4.0 License. 





- 654 Our analysis of protected area exposure is a new addition to this year’s report, with the 655 approach  adapted from the published work of Resco de Dios et al. (2025). The exposures 656 are reported for the regions listed in Table 1 and for the March-February fire-season definition. 657 Protected-area polygons were compiled primarily from the World Database on Protected 658 Areas (WDPA), supplemented with national datasets where WDPA coverage was restricted 659 or incomplete, and cleaned to remove overlaps so that co-located designations were not 660 counted repeatedly. Protected areas were treated as multipart sites, meaning that multiple 661 spatial fragments with the same site identifier were analysed as a single protected area. 662 Following Resco de Dios et al. (2025), analyses were restricted to natural and semi-natural 663 burnable vegetation using a wildland land-cover mask, and regions with unavailable or 664 insufficient protected-area data were excluded. Annual MODIS MCD64A1 Collection 6.1 BA 665 (Giglio et al., 2018) was then intersected with the protected area layer to calculate, for each 666 reporting region and fire season, the number and fraction of protected areas exposed to fire, 667 the fraction of total protected area extent burned, and associated relative and standard 668 anomalies. 669 670 The data produced using these methods are available from Cunill Camprubí et al. (2026). 671 

### 672 **2.2.5. Forest Carbon Project Exposure Assessment** 

673 674 Our approach to calculating forest carbon project exposure assessment is largely unchanged 675 from last year, aside from the geographical unit of reporting which now maps onto the regional 676 layers provided in Table 1. We assessed fire exposure across 1,798 forestry-based carbon 677 offset projects using digitised project boundaries from BeZero Carbon Ltd., spanning  South 678 America (n=324), North America (n=905), Asia (n=228), Africa (n=157), Europe (172) and 679 Oceania (n=12). These projects are a subset of the total number of forest carbon project 680 boundaries digitised by BeZero Carbon Ltd., after excluding JREDD+ projects and project 681 areas larger than 70,000 km<sup>2</sup> (a total of 33 projects), which often span multiple countries or 682 entire continents and are therefore not comparable with spatially discrete forest carbon 683 projects. Annual BA was derived from MODIS MCD64A1 Collection 6.1 and intersected with 684 MODIS MCD12Q1 land cover to separate forest and non-forest fire activity (Giglio et al., 2018; 685 Sulla-Menashe et al., 2019). Drought conditions were assessed using 12-month Standardised 686 Precipitation-Evapotranspiration Index (SPEI) calculated from ERA5-Land and calibrated over 687 1980–2014 (Muñoz-Sabater et al., 2021). For each year since 2002, we calculated the number 688 of projects affected by fire and the mean percentage of project area burned. To place this in 689 the context of climate change, we calculated the 2025 drought anomaly as the 2025 SPEI 690 minus the long-term average SPEI (1980–2024). The results from this drought analysis are 691 presented in Supplement S2.4. 692 693 The data produced using these methods are available from Andela et al. (2026). 694 

## 695 **2.3. Shortlisting of Focal Events** 

- 696 

- 697 We selected three focal events for detailed analysis of the drivers and impacts of extreme 698 wildfire activity during March 2025-February 2026. As in our previous assessments, the 699 number of focal events was limited to a computationally manageable set of internationally 700 significant episodes. Events were shortlisted in consultation with the continental expert panels, 701 considering both the magnitude of anomalies in Earth observations, the extremity of fire extent, 702 emissions or behaviour versus fire regime contexts, and the severity of societal and 703 environmental impacts deriving either from our global impact assessments or from other 704 sources accessed by the expert panels. Across our reports, focal events have been selected 705 for their scientific and international significance, rather than solely for the level of international 706 media attention they received. The specific motivations for selecting each focal event are 707 presented in Sections 4, 5, and 6.

759 interpretability. 

- 760 Period-attribution metrics are obtained by integrating these regionalised perturbations over 761 time. Values greater than one indicate enhancement of climatological BA associated with 762 anomalous driver conditions, while values below one indicate suppression relative to typical 763 seasonal behaviour. These diagnostics describe sensitivities within the model representation 764 rather than causal attribution of observed BA, but they provide a physically interpretable 765 decomposition of the factors associated with anomalous modelled fire-season activity. 

## 766 **2.5. Influence of Climate Change on Focal Events** 

- 767 

768 As an update to last year’s report, and working towards a more integrated and seamless 769 attribution approach, we focus on attributing changes in fire weather and BA due to climate 770 change for our three focal extreme events, and present the attribution analysis alongside 771 projected changes in fire characteristics with future climate change. Linking both present-day 772 and future attribution analysis in this way enables a more evidence-driven approach to the 773 results. 

- 774 

### 775 **2.5.1. Attribution of Present Day Focal Events - FWI** 

776 

777 To assess the influence of climate change on fire weather for the observed focal events, we 778 calculate the Canadian Fire Weather Index (FWI) as presented in previous State of Wildfire 779 reports (Jones et al., 2024b; Kelley et al., 2025). This uses daily temperature, mean relative 780 humidity, 10 m wind speed, and total daily precipitation. 781 

782 This year, we follow the multi-model method from the World Weather Attribution Protocol, 783 described in Philip et al. (2020), as follows. We calculate the “observed” FWI using ERA5 784 reanalysis data (Hersbach et al., 2020) for the month and region of the focal event (our ‘event 785 definition’, as defined in Section 3.3.7). We then calculate the FWI across all CMIP6 (Eyring 786 et al., 2016) and HighResMIP (Haarsma et al. 2016) models (labelled ‘models’). We assess 787 the performance of each climate model and ensemble member regionally using a qualitative 788 comparison of the seasonal cycle and spatial pattern for a 1990-2019 climatology, and the 789 parameters of the fitted statistical models. If the model does not pass in any one of these three 790 criteria, it is excluded from the analysis (see Supplement S1.2.1) for full model evaluation 791 details). For each time series, we fit a Generalised Extreme Value (GEV) model, scaled by a 792 4-year smoothed Global Surface Air Temperature (GSAT). This allows us to compare the 793 climate of the preindustrial past (1850-1900, representing our ‘counterfactual’ scenario) to the 794 climate of today (calculated as 1.3°C warmer, based on the Global Warming Index (Leedham 795 et al. 2026), representing our ‘factual’ scenario). We do the same for reanalysis products 796 (ERA5 for all focal regions, and MERRA2 for Northwest Iberia). The reanalysis and models 797 are combined to give the synthesis; a best estimate following the method of Otto et al. (2024). 798 We calculate the Probability Ratio (PR), defined as the ratio of the probability of an FWI event 799 with the observed return period occurring in the factual vs counterfactual climate, across all 800 models and reanalyses. 801 802 The CMIP6 ensemble includes 9 models (CanESM5, CNRM-ESM2-1, GFDL-CM4, MIROC6, 803 MIP-ESM1-2-LR, MRI-ESM2-0, AWI-ESM-1-REcoM, INM-CM4-8 and INM-CM5-0) and 804 HighResMIP includes 7 models (CNRM-CM6-1-HR, CNRM-CM6-1, EC-Earth3P-HR, EC805 Earth3P, HadGEM3-GC31-HM, HadGEM3-GC31-LM, HadGEM3-GC31-MM). We bootstrap 806 the models 1000 times to estimate the upper and lower bounds of a 95% confidence interval 807 for each. For all CMIP6 simulations, the period 1850 to 2015 is based on historical simulations, 808 while the Shared Socioeconomic Pathway (SSP) 5-8.5 scenario is used for the remainder of 809 the 21st century (as per Philip et al., 2022). HighResMIP is a Sea Surface Temperature (SST)810 forced model ensemble (Haarsma et al. 2016) with simulations covering 1950-2050. The SST 

18 

https://doi.org/10.5194/essd-2026-587 Preprint. Discussion started: 3 August 2026 _⃝_ c Author(s) 2026. CC BY 4.0 License. 





- 811 and sea ice forcings for the period 1950-2014 are obtained from the 0.25° x 0.25° Hadley 812 Centre Global Sea Ice and Sea Surface Temperature reanalysis dataset (HadISST; Rayner 813 et al., 2003), regridded to match the climate model resolution. For the current/future time 814 period (2015-2050), SST/sea-ice data are derived from RCP8.5 (CMIP5) data, and combined 815 with greenhouse gas forcings from SSP5-8.5 (CMIP6) simulations (Haarsma et al. 2016). 816 817 As an additional line of model evidence, we also use the HadGEM3-A large ensemble 818 (Ciavarella et al., 2018) to calculate the PR for the observed focal event, as in previous reports. 819 This configuration runs in near real time and is designed to enable specific event attribution 820 studies such as this. It provides a 525-member ensemble with observed SSTs and all 821 anthropogenic forcing for the factual simulation, and a 525-member ensemble with natural822 only forcing (solar and volcanic activity) for the counterfactual simulation, allowing direct 823 attribution of observed events to anthropogenic climate forcing. This system is currently 824 undergoing an upgrade, and only data up to and including 2024 is now available. We therefore 825 run our analysis for the 2025-2026 events using 2020-2024 factual and counterfactual data to 826 represent a near-present-day climate state. Using a 5 year period reduces SST influence from 827 any individual year. We present the results alongside the multi-model assessment outlined 828 above. Methods follow last year's report; in short, we first evaluate and bias correct the model 829 data to ERA5 over the historical period (here we select the period 1980-2013 to avoid 830 uncertainties in satellite data pre-1980, see Supplement S1.2.1), and apply this correction to 831 the full present day attribution ensemble. We calculate a PR from the factual and 832 counterfactual ensembles, and bootstrap to give uncertainty ranges. Both the WWA and 833 HadGEM3-A ensemble analyses specifically target the months identified as extreme for each 834 focal event as outlined in Section 3.3.7, and focus on sub-regional extremes that occur in the 835 model grid cells with the highest FWI values temporally and spatially (top 5% of all regional 836 grid cells during the period). 837 838 In addition to the 95<sup>th</sup> percentile (FWI95), we also calculate the FWI7X (the 7 days of highest 839 FWI during the focal event period), as an additional measure of extreme fire weather, from the 840 output of 3 CMIP6 ESMs that have large ensembles available (CanESM5 (50 members), 841 ACCESS-ESM1-5 (30 members), and MPI-ESM1-2-LR (10 members) (Gallo et al., 2023)). A 842 GEV scaling method is applied to the whole ensemble for each model based on Liu et al. 843 (2025), and a 1000-sample non-parametric bootstrapping method is used to give uncertainty 844 estimates for the FWI PRs. While FWI95 gives a reasonable upper bound for the peak intensity 845 of an event by looking at the 95th percentile value of the FWI during the period, FWI7X gives 846 additional context of the intensity by looking at the maxima of running 7 day FWI mean (Liu et 847 al., 2025). While both metrics look at the peak/near peak values, FWI7X tempers this by 848 reviewing local maxima in the FWI series. A high value of FWI95 with a correspondingly lower 849 value of FWI7X suggests the anomaly was concentrated in time, while a relatively lower FWI95 850 with a higher FWI7X suggests a longer, high period of sustained fire weather. As both are 851 relevant metrics with complementary use cases and context, particularly when reviewing the 852 causes for sustained drawn-out burns versus a fast-moving quick burn, both metrics were 853 used to calculate a PR. As the wider community has indicated useful interpretation of both, 854 they are provided to enable commonalities between events to be compared for multiple lines 855 of evidence. 856 857 Using three different approaches here allows us to target different aspects of attribution, while 858 looking at the same extreme event. The WWA approach represents the degree to which the 859 observed FWI could be expected in today’s climate compared to the pre-industrial past 860 (emergence from natural variability); HadGEM3-A targets a specific event while controlling 861 internal variability (event-specific attribution); the CMIP6 large ensemble reports the strength 862 of the climate change signal (external forcing) on an event like 2025-26. 863

864 **2.5.2. Future Projections - FWI** 865 866 For the projections of future fire weather, we follow the same calculation of FWI as for the 867 WWA multi-model attribution analysis (using CMIP6 and HighResMIP models), but this time 868 compare FWI at Global Warming Levels (GWLs) +1.5°C, +2.0°C, and +3.0°C above pre869 industrial relative to a present-day value taken as +1.3°C. It is important to note that all models 870 reach +3.0°C warming. We again supplement this with FWI7X calculated across the same 871 large ensemble CMIP6 models for all GWLs. 872 873 Additional metrics calculated for the FWI seasonal average _fwisa_ , defined by the local annual 874 maximum of the 90-day running average of the FWI and the annual maximum _fwixx_ , defined 875 by the annual maximum value of FWI (Quilcaille et al., 2023; Gallo et al., 2025). Projections 876 for _fwisa_ and _fwixx_ from CMIP6 models are presented in the Supplement for each focal region, 877 and for three climate change scenarios defined by SSP1-2.6, SSP2-4.5 and SSP5-8.5 (Riahi 878 et al., 2017). SSP1-2.6 represents strong mitigation consistent with the Paris Agreement, 879 limiting warming to around 2°C and aiming for 1.5°C. SSP2-4.5 represents an intermediate 880 scenario in a world with both adaptation and mitigation. SSP5-8.5, originally developed in 2008 881 as a high-end, no-mitigation scenario, is now inconsistent with the evolution of our subsequent 882 emissions trajectory and will be discontinued in CMIP7 (Van Vuuren et al., 2026); however, it 883 is retained here to illustrate avoided impacts and the comparison between lower emissions 884 pathways, and provides context for how mitigation has already shifted expected outcomes 885 relative to earlier worst-case assumptions. 886 887 **2.5.3. Attribution of Present Day Focal Events - BA** 888 889 As per the last report, we use ConFLAME (Controles de Fogo Local Analisados pela Máxima 890 Entropia - English “Local Fire Control Analysis by Maximum Entropy”; Kelley et al., 2021; 891 Barbosa et al., 2025) to attribute the likelihood and extent of the observed 2025-2026 BA 892 during focal months to anthropogenic climate forcing. Unlike most process-based fire models, 893 ConFLAME does not explicitly simulate the physical mechanisms of fire; instead, it infers the 894 influence of environmental controls on BA from observed relationships within a probabilistic 895 framework, learning from observed patterns between drivers and BA. Controls sort driving 896 variables into fuel continuity (from here on “fuel”), weather or driver variables including tree 897 cover (“dryness”), anthropogenic and natural ignitions (“ignitions”) and suppression, both 898 active and fragmentation (“suppression”). ConFLAME is designed to capture the inherent 899 stochasticity in fire activity, arising from the intrinsically variable nature of fire itself and from 900 the fact that key drivers of BA are only partially observed, or often unresolved, in large-scale 901 datasets. These two sources of uncertainty are particularly important for extreme events, 902 which lie in the tails of the distribution and are often poorly captured by deterministic or mean903 state modelling approaches. ConFLAME addresses this using a Bayesian framework, 904 applying a large ensemble approach to represent the full distribution of possible BA outcomes, 905 by sampling from relationships learned from historical observations. This allows the full range 906 of plausible outcomes, including extremes, to be represented and analysed probabilistically in 907 terms of likelihood and amplification. 908 909 We trained ConFLAME using ERA5 reanalysis data (Muñoz-Sabater et al., 2021) to represent 910 the meteorological conditions during the observed fire events, together with land-cover 911 fractions from ISAM-HYDE (Ciavarella et al., 2018) (mapped to tree, shrub, and grass cover, 912 consistent with the HadGEM3-A configuration used in the FWI attribution) and HYDE land use 913 (Klein Goldewijk et al., 2010). The model was optimized against MODIS BA (see Section 914 2.1.2.1) following the same bayesian inference approach as in previous reports (Kelley et al., 915 2025). We then applied the optimised model using two sets of inputs: factual and 916 counterfactual conditions. The factual simulations are driven directly by ERA5 and therefore 917 represent the real-world conditions under which the fires occurred. The counterfactual 918 simulations are constructed by removing the influence of anthropogenic climate change from

First, the amplification factor (AF) quantifies how much larger (or smaller)<br>BA is for events of equivalent rarity, calculated as the ratio of our factual BA to our<br>counterfactual BA. For attributing our focal event directly (i.e. Sections 4.3.2, 5.3.2 and 6.3.2),|
|938<br>939|the factual and counterfactual experiments are taken from the month(s) of the events, and are<br>conditioned on the observed BA (see Supplement S1.2.2 for calculation), whereas for annual|
|940<br>941<br>942|<br>average BA, the factual and counterfactual use annual average BA over all months, and are<br>not conditioned on observed BA. We also calculated how much larger (AF) BA was due to fuel<br>or dryness alone. Second, the Probability Ratio (PR) quantifies how much more likely an event|
|943<br>944<br>945|with comparable BA is under factual than under counterfactual conditions. We do this by<br>calculating the ratio of probability of the observed BA, given the BA simulated by paired factual<br>and counterfactual ensemble members. ConFLAME gives the likelihood of all possible BA|
|946|<br>outcomes for each region based on a probabilistic analysis of past burn patterns and|
|947<br>948|environmental conditions. Each result, therefore, presents a 5-95th percentile probability<br>estimate which we refer to as the uncertaint rane We also reort the likelihood of AF bein|
|<br>949<br>950|y g.   p     g<br>greater than 1 - i.e., the likelihood that anthropogenic climate forcing led to an increase in BA.|
|951<br>952|**2.5.4.**<br>**Future Projections - BA**|
|953<br>954<br>955<br>956<br>|Our approach follows that used in last year’s report but with the addition of AF to align with<br>the attribution framework. We use the same ConFLAME model as for the attribution analysis,<br>though using different training and experiment datasets, including bias-corrected climate<br>projections.|
|957||
|<br>958<br>959|ConFLAME was trained on GSWP3 relanalysis data available via Inter-Sectoral Impact Model<br>Intercomparison Project version 3a (ISIMIP3a) (Frieler et al., 2024) and MODIS VCF|
|960|<br>(Vegetation Continuous Fields) fractional vegetation cover as per last year's report (Kelley et|
|961|al. 2025). For projections, we took climate projections from ISIMIP3b (Frieler et al., 2026),|
|962|which provides bias-corrected global climate model (GCM) outputs for historical (up to 2014)|
|963<br>964<br>965|and current/future (2015-2100) periods. Bias correction ensures a seamless transition<br>between historical and future. We use five GCMs (GFDL-ESM4, IPSL-CM6A-LR, MPI-ESM1-<br>2-HR, MRI-ESM2-0, and UKESM1-0-LL), each bias-corrected to W5E5 (a merged WFDE5|
|966<br>967|<br>and ERA5 reanalysis dataset) following Lange (2019). ConFLAME is trained using the<br>ISIMIP3a GSWP3 forcing dataset, while simulations are driven by GCM outputs (ISIMIP3b)|
|968|bias-corrected to WFDE5 ERA5 To quantif the influence of these differences on baseline-|
|<br>969<br>|.  y<br>period results, an analysis is included in Kelley et al. (2026) to assess the sensitivity of model<br>|
|970|outputs to these forcing choices. Vegetation and fuel availability are simulated offline using|
|971<br>|<br>JULES-ES (the terrestrial Earth system component of UKESM) forced by these climate data<br>|
|972|and scenario-specific CO₂concentrations (Mathison et al. 2023), with resulting land cover|

|974<br>975<br>976<br>977|(Spuler et al., 2024). Future socioeconomic drivers, such as changes in ignition or land use,<br>are set at present-day conditions, so that we simulate the effects of climate only, which<br>includes climate influence over vegetation cover and resultant fuel connectivity.|
|978<br>979<br>980<br>981<br>982|Projections are performed under three SSPs: SSP1-2.6 and SSP5-8.5 as defined above, and<br>SSP3-7.0 which represents a high-emissions pathway broadly consistent with, though at the<br>higher end of, our current global policies and is used here as a pessimistic but plausible<br>trajectory.|

AF and PR are calculated as per attribution in section 2.5.3, though with<br>2010-2019 taking the place of our counterfactual and the target decade our factual. For<br>subsections 4.3.2, 5.3.2 and 6.3.2, we defined AF as changes in BA for events with the same<br>likelihood as the 2025-2026 event (defined by ConFLAME simulations), estimated using|

|991|<br>the regional scale (whole focal region) and for sub-regional extremes (top 5% BA grid cells).|
|992<br>993<br>994<br>995|We test the significance of the change in the resultant 5 AFs (one for each GCM) relative to<br>the baseline-period AFs using a paired permutation/sign-flip test, appropriate for small-<br>ensemble significance testing (see the Supplement for details).|
|996<br>997|**2.5.5.**<br>**Future Projections - Typical Fire Behaviour**|
|998<br>999|While future projections of BA are not yet available from the full set of FireMIP models under<br>the ISIMIP3b protocol, we performed future simulations with the data-driven model BuRNN|
|1000<br>1001<br>1002|(“Burned area modelling through Recurrent Neural Network”; Lampe et al., 2026) using the<br>ISIMIP3b driving data (Frieler et al., 2026). BuRNN is trained to predict the BA of GFED5 from<br>a range of climatic, vegetation, and socio-economic parameters at a 0.5° by 0.5° spatial scale|
|1003<br>1004<br>|<br>and a monthly time resolution. BuRNN exists out of 55 Long Short-Term Memory networks<br>(LSTMs) that are trained on different splits of AR6 regions. Predictions in a specific<br>|
|1005|region/gridcell are the mean of 5 LSTMs that have not seen data for that entire AR6 region|
|<br>1006<br>1007<br>1008|<br>before, neither in training nor in validation. Here, only two minor changes were made in the<br>methodology of Lampe et al. (2026). First, we trained on the updated version of GFED5, i.e.,<br>GFED5.1. Second, instead of taking the multi-model mean of Gross Primary Productivity|
|1009<br>1010|(GPP), Leaf Area Index (LAI) and carbon mass stored in vegetation (Cveg) from all ISIMIP<br>models we used the values from the model VISIT (Ito 2019). We did the latter as ISIMIP3b|
|<br>1011|,         ,<br>consists of many experiment setups and not all models have performed all experiments. At|
|1012<br>1013<br>1014|<br>the time of the analysis, VISIT had the highest number of experiment results available. We<br>then force this version of BuRNN with three SSPs (SSP1-2.6, SSP3-7.0 and SSP5-8.5)<br>considering only a single bias-corrected ESM (GFDL-ESM4) and land-use allocation model|
|<br>1015|<br>(Integrated Model to Assess the Global Environment IMAGE Stehfest et al. 2014). The|
|<br>1016<br>1017|, ,   ,<br>simulations were performed with the SSP-associated climate and socio-economic forcings.|
|1018<br>1019|We assessed how well different historical projections of BA, fire size, and fire intensity<br>performed in each focal region under present-day conditions and, where appropriate, the|
|1020<br>1021|agreement in the directionality and magnitude of their future projections. For this, we obtained<br>the outputs of generalized linear model (GLM) simulations of future fire activity (Haas et al.|
|1022<br>1023<br>1024<br>|<br>2026). The models used in this study were originally developed by Haas et al. (2022) and<br>represent three independent models for BA, fire size, and a metric of fire intensity, primarily<br>driven by climate drivers but also accounting for human activity and vegetation effects on fire.<br>|
|1025|The models were designed and perform best as a timestep and they represent the average|
|<br>1026<br>|,<br>spatial conditions in fire regimes. In order to minimise the uncertainty in projections, we<br>|
|1027|compared the 2010-2020 period with the 2090-2100 period varying only the climate predictors|

|1030|2020 baseline conditions. Importantly, for consistency in potential land cover and vegetation|
|1031|effects, vegetation predictors were derived from BIOME4 simulations (Kaplan et al., 2003) and|

|1045|**3.**<br>**Extreme Wildfire Events and their Impacts in 2025-26**|
|---|---|
|1046||
|1047<br>|**3.1.**<br>**Global Summary: BA and Fire Carbon Emissions Anomalies**|
|1048||
|1049|According to the MODIS BA product, at least 3.29 million km<sup>2</sup>burned globally during the 2025-|
|1050|26 global fire season (March 2025-February 2026), 18% below the average of previous fire|
|1051|seasons since 2002 (Figure 1, Figure 2, Table 4). This ranks 23rd of 24 fire seasons in the|
|1052|MODIS record, indicating one of the lowest global BA totals observed since 2002. Fire carbon|
|1053<br>|emissions were also below average globally (Figure 1, Figure 3, Table 5). GFED5.1 fire carbon<br>|
|1054|emissions were 2.74 PgC, 682 TgC below average, with relative anomalies of -20% and -31%|
|1055|<br>in GFED5.1 and GFAS, respectively. The 2025-2026 fire season therefore, departed from the|
|1056|<br>pattern seen in the preceding two fire seasons, when above-average fire carbon emissions|
|1057|occurred despite below-average global BA.|
|1058||
|1059|The low global BA total was in line with the ongoing decline in BA across major savannah and|
|1060<br>|grassland systems, which continue to account for a large share of global BA. The global<br>|
|1061<br>|(sub)tropical grassland, savannah and shrubland biome burned 2.44 million km<sup>2</sup>, 406,000 km<sup>2</sup><br>|
|1062|(14%) below average and the second-lowest annual total in the MODIS record. GFED5.1 fire|
|1063|<br>carbon emissions from this biome were 1.37 PgC, 224 TgC below average, with relative|
|1064|<br>anomalies of -14% and -39% in GFED5.1 and GFAS, respectively. The largest negative|
|1065|anomaly contribution came from the African (sub)tropical grassland, savannah and shrubland|
|1066|biome, where 1.98 million km<sup>2</sup>burned, 341,000 km<sup>2</sup>(15%) below average and the lowest|
|1067<br>1068<br>1069|annual total since 2002. BA was also strongly below average across Asia and South America,<br>which both recorded their lowest continental BA totals in the MODIS record.|
|1070<br>1071|Despite this globally low fire year, stark regional contrasts were visible across the Earth<br>observations. The clearest positive fire carbon emissions anomaly occurred in North America,|
|1072<br>1073|where BA was only moderately above average but GFED5.1 fire carbon emissions reached<br>418 TgC, 177 TgC above average, with relative anomalies of +74% and +81% in GFED5.1|
|1074<br>|and GFAS, respectively. This anomaly was concentrated in the North American boreal forest,<br>|
|1075|principally in central Canada, where BA and emissions were again among the highest values|
|1076<br>1077|<br>on record. The 2025-2026 season therefore marked a third consecutive year of extreme fire<br>activity in North American boreal forests, although the geographical focus shifted from the|
|1078<br>1079|<br>western and northwestern regions emphasised in recent fire seasons towards central Canada.|
|1080<br>1081|Outside North America, most major positive anomalies were more regional or subregional than<br>continental in scale. In Africa, the continent as a whole recorded its lowest MODIS BA total,|
|1082|<br>but this masked anomalous burning in the Sahel and semi-arid West Africa, especially Sudan,|
|1083|<br>Niger and Burkina Faso, as well as a record BA season in the Western Cape of South Africa.|
|1084|<br>In Asia, BA and fire carbon emissions were both among the lowest on record, but positive BA|
|1085|anomalies were concentrated in South, Southeast and East Asia, including Myanmar, India,|
|1086|Pakistan, Bangladesh and South Korea. South Korea was particularly notable because it|
|1087|recorded both its highest MODIS BA and highest fire carbon emissions in both emissions|
|<br>1088|<br>products.|
|<br>1089||
|1090|Europe also presents a scale-dependent picture. Continental BA was below average in our|
|1091|<br>BA analysis, and the two emissions products differed in the sign of the relative continental|
|1092|<br>emissions anomaly, but the Mediterranean region experienced a clearly anomalous season.<br>|
|1093|The European Mediterranean forests, woodlands and scrub biome burned 7,339 km<sup>2</sup>, 3,865<br>|
|1094|km<sup>2</sup>(111%) above average and Spain recorded its highest national MODIS BA total since the|
|<br>1095|,<br>start of the record in 2002. The United Kingdom was also a notable north-west European|
|1096|<br>outlier, with its highest MODIS BA and highest GFED5.1 fire carbon emissions in the record,|
|1097|<br>although absolute BA remained small compared with southern Europe. Notably, records from|



24 

https://doi.org/10.5194/essd-2026-587 Preprint. Discussion started: 3 August 2026 _⃝_ c Author(s) 2026. CC BY 4.0 License. 





|1098<br>1099<br>1100<br>1101<br>1102<br>1103<br>1104<br>1105<br>1106<br>1107|the European Forest Fire Information System (2026; EFFIS) suggest a different situation, with<br>19,629 km<sup>2</sup>burned across Europe in 2025-26, the largest total since its records began in 2006<br>and 301% above the dataset’s 2006-2024 average (Section 3.3.3). This is explained by<br>differences in the temporal and spatial coverage of the EFFIS system versus our definition of<br>the European continent used here (ArcGIS Hub, 2024). European EFFIS coverage is<br>restricted to the 27 EU member states plus Albania, Bosnia and Herzegovina, Kosovo,<br>Montenegro, North Macedonia, Norway, Serbia, Switzerland, Türkiye, the UK, and Ukraine,<br>whereas the adopted continents layer also includes western parts of Russia (west of the Ural<br>mountains) and Moldova.<br>|
|---|---|
|1108<br>1109|In Australia, 477,000 km<sup>2</sup>burned, which is the largest positive national BA anomaly globally<br>in absolute terms, but the national total ranked only tenth in the MODIS record and fire carbon|
|1110<br>1111|emissions were close to or below average depending on the emissions product. This supports<br>the interpretation of the 2025-2026 Australian season as one characterised by widespread fire|
|1112<br>1113<br>1114|occurrence and large fires in northern savanna and arid-zone systems, rather than an<br>exceptional national emissions year. South America, by contrast, experienced a much lower<br>fire season than in 2024-2025: continental BA and fire carbon emissions were both around|
|1115<br>1116<br>1117|half below average. However, localised positive anomalies occurred in Chaparral and<br>temperate forest regions of Chile and Argentina (see Section 6), as well as in parts of Brazil.|
|1118<br>1119<br>1120<br>1121|Overall, the 2025-2026 global fire season was defined less by globally elevated fire activity<br>than by strong contrasts across spatial scales. Global BA and fire carbon emissions were<br>among the lowest in the satellite record, driven by suppressed burning in several extensive<br>savannah and grassland regions. Within that low global context, severe regional fire anomalies|
|1122|occurred in central boreal Canada, the Sahel and semi-arid West Africa, South and East Asia,|
|1123|the European Mediterranean region and United Kingdom, northern and arid Australia, and|
|1124<br>1125|parts of southern South America. This pattern highlights the importance of assessing fire<br>extremes across multiple regional layers: global and continental totals identify a low fire year,|
|1126<br>1127<br>1128<br>1129|while biome, national and subnational layers reveal several regionally severe fire seasons with<br>important ecological and societal consequences.|

In section (b), other administrative regions belonging to 1180 unlisted countries are shown. In section (c), the anomalies for selected countries are shown 1181 due to their nationally high-ranking and large relative anomalies. Estimates of fire carbon 1182 emissions or absolute anomalies in the 2025-2026 derive from the GFED5 emissions product, 1183 whereas for relative anomalies, we primarily report our synthesis values with the GFED5 1184 values reported in parentheses (see Section 2.1.2.3). Extended data for all countries and 1185 region layers are available from Jones et al. (2026b). Countries are not shown if the sign of 1186 the relative carbon emission anomaly differs between GFED5.1 and CAMS GFAS, reflecting 1187 low confidence in the <u>direction of the anomaly.</u>

1446 Beyond the insights captured by the regional panel report, we report on indicators of extreme 1447 fire behaviour within Africa. Our analyses show that the broad continental suppression of BA 1448 and fire carbon emissions was also reflected in subdued fire intensity across the major 1449 savannah systems, while several Sahelian regions showed clear evidence of unusual fire 1450 occurrence, size or growth: 1451 ● Peak fire radiative intensity in the African (sub)tropical grasslands, savannahs and 1452 shrubland biomes was 11% below average and the lowest in the record. 1453 ● Sudan’s positive BA anomaly was linked mainly to widespread fire occurrence: the 1454 number of mapped fires was 189% above average, but 95th percentile fire size and 1455 peak intensity were below average. 1456 ● Niger recorded its highest 95th percentile rate of growth in the record, 64% above 1457 average. 1458 ● Tahoua, Niger, had exceptionally large and fast-growing fires, with 95th percentile fire 1459 size 332% above average and 95th percentile rate of growth 232% above average. 1460 ● Burkina Faso’s Sahel region recorded its highest 95th percentile fire size, 387% above 1461 average. 1462 ● Centre-Nord, Burkina Faso, recorded its highest number of mapped fires, 1113% 1463 above average, together with strongly elevated fire size and growth metrics. 1464 ● The Western Cape’s record BA season was supported by elevated peak fire radiative 1465 intensity, 26% above average, though 95th percentile fire size and rate of growth were 1466 less exceptional. These emissions are far lower than in the last drought where the 1467 emissions were nearly double average for four years (2016-2019). 1468 1469 **3.3.2. Asia** 1470 1471 Based on the regional MODIS BA and GFED fire carbon emissions analyses, the 2025-2026 1472 fire season in Asia had significant positive anomalies across South, Southeast and East Asia 1473 despite below-average continental totals. The expert-panel report estimates 235,112 km2 1474 burned across Asia in MODIS MCD64A1, 38% below the post-2002 average, with GFED fire 1475 emissions 49% below average. Regional MODIS anomalies show record or near-record BA 1476 in Myanmar, India, Pakistan, Bangladesh and South Korea. (2026) and included: 1478 ● Myanmar recorded Asia’s highest absolute BA anomaly in the regional MODIS 1479 analysis, at approximately +11,000 km<sup>2</sup> , concentrated in Chin State (+265%; highest 1480 since 2002). 1481 ● India recorded its third-highest BA season since 2002 in the regional MODIS analysis, 1482 at approximately +9,300 km<sup>2</sup> above average, with record burning in Mizoram (+1015%) 1483 and the fourth-highest season in Madhya Pradesh. 1484 ● Pakistan and Bangladesh recorded their highest national BA seasons since 2002 in 1485 the regional MODIS analysis: Pakistan was approximately +2,500 km<sup>2</sup> above average 1486 (+120%), while Bangladesh was approximately +2,000 km<sup>2</sup> above average (+412%). 1487 ● South Korea recorded its highest BA season since 2002 in the regional MODIS 1488 analysis, at approximately +880 km<sup>2</sup> above average (+896%), the largest relative BA 1489 anomaly globally. GFED ranked South Korea second globally for relative fire carbon 1490 emissions anomaly (+329%). 1491 ● The South Korean spring 2025 fires were the deadliest in the national record, with 32 1492 fatalities, 54 injuries, approximately 37,000 residents evacuated and nearly 5,000 1493 structures destroyed. During 21-30 March, 79 wildfires burned under temperatures 1494 more than 10°C above average, relative humidity below 20%, and winds exceeding 15 1495 m s<sup>-1</sup> . 1496 ● In Pakistan, catastrophic fires in Balochistan caused at least 35 fatalities, burned 1497 thousands of hectares of forest and displaced more than 3,000 families. 1498 ● Myanmar’s 2025-26 BA was 33% above the 2002-2024 average in MODIS MCD64A1 1499 according to the expert-panel report, with agricultural and forest fires, especially in 1500 Shan State, contributing to transboundary haze affecting Thailand and Laos.

### 1532 **3.3.3. Europe** 

1533 Based on the regional MODIS BA and GFED fire carbon emissions analyses, Europe 1534 experienced its most extreme wildfire season in the observational record during 2025-2026 1535 according to regional fire monitoring products, with record BA and fire carbon emissions 1536 concentrated in the Iberian Peninsula. The European Forest Fire Information System (EFFIS) 1537 estimated 19,629 km<sup>2</sup> burned across Europe in 2025-26, the largest total since its records 1538 began in 2006 and 301% above the 2006-2024 average. EFFIS also estimated 58.35 Mt CO2 1539 emissions, 165% above its reference-period mean (European Forest Fire Information System, 1540 2026), while the regional GFED analysis indicated record fire carbon emissions for Europe. 1541 As noted in Section 3.1, there is some misalignment between the EFFIS data presented here 1542 and our analysis of the global MODIS BA product, which conversely showed below-average 1543 BA on the European continent. This is explained by differences in the temporal and spatial 1544 coverage of the EFFIS system versus our definition of the European continent used here 1545 (ArcGIS Hub, 2024; see Section 3.1 for details). 

- 1546 The key events of the 2025-26 fire season are summarised by Fernandes et al. (2026) and 1547 included: 

- 1548 ● EFFIS recorded 7,205 km<sup>2</sup> burned across Europe during two exceptional August 1549 weeks. During 8-26 August 2025, approximately 5,247 km<sup>2</sup> burned in the Iberian 1550 Peninsula across 66 large fires exceeding 5 km<sup>2</sup> . The event coincided with Spain’s 1551 warmest summer since 1961 and driest in the region most affected, Portugal’s warmest 

40

- 1552 and driest June-August period since 1931, and first-ranked August FWI values across 1553 much of the Northwest Iberian Peninsula since 1985. 1554 ● Spain was the dominant contributor to the continent’s positive fire anomaly seen in our 1555 MODIS BA analysis (Section 2.1.2), recording its highest national BA since 2002, with 1556 approximately 3,600 km<sup>2</sup> burned and a +266% anomaly. EFFIS BA totals for Spain 1557 were comparable, at 3,931 km<sup>2</sup> . Nine Spanish fires exceeded 100 km<sup>2</sup> and burned in 1558 just 15 days over 13-28th August, including the Molezuelas de la Carballeda fire in 1559 Zamora, which burned 400 km<sup>2</sup> and became the largest wildfire recorded in Spain. 1560 ● Portugal recorded a +120% BA anomaly in the regional MODIS analysis, while EFFIS 1561 reported around 2,789 km<sup>2</sup> burned, equivalent to 3.0% of national land area. Six 1562 Portuguese fires exceeded 100 km<sup>2</sup> (the 10,000 ha threshold commonly used to define 1563 a “megafire”), including the Arganil fire, which burned 654.17 km<sup>2</sup> and became the 1564 largest wildfire recorded in Portugal. 1565 ● Spain reported eight fatalities, 86 injuries, 42,913 evacuations and 108,202 people 1566 confined. Portugal reported six fatalities, at least 246 hospitalisations, 308 people 1567 assisted on site, and 16 activations of the community-based wildfire evacuation and 1568 preparedness program “Safe Villages, Safe People”. 1569 ● The United Kingdom recorded its highest national BA season since 2002 in the 1570 regional MODIS analysis, with a +288% anomaly, and ranked among the largest 1571 positive relative fire carbon emissions anomalies globally in the regional emissions 1572 analyses (+121-169%). The Dava-Carrbridge fire complex in the Scottish Highlands 1573 burned approximately 100 km<sup>2</sup> , making it the UK’s first recorded “megafire” according 1574 to a 100 km<sup>2</sup> (10,000 ha) definition. High-severity burning affected more than 80% of 1575 the Dava-Carrbridge fire complex, with peat combustion estimated to contribute 85% 1576 of the fire’s total carbon emissions. Meanwhile, the Abergwesyn fire in Wales burned 1577 more than 50 km<sup>2</sup> . 1578 ● Albania recorded its second-highest BA season in the regional MODIS analysis 1579 (+202%). EFFIS identified the Western Balkans as the second major European 1580 hotspot, with 2,250 km<sup>2</sup> burned, including Albania’s Dhivër fire, which burned 105.65 1581 km<sup>2</sup> and was the largest recorded in the country. 1582 ● Cyprus recorded a national record BA season in EFFIS, including the Limassol fire, 1583 which burned 101 km<sup>2</sup> , killed two people and damaged 532 houses, 113 warehouses, 1584 29 businesses and 300 vehicles. 1585 ● France’s Ribaute fire burned 113.91 km<sup>2</sup> in less than eight hours, leading to 1 fatality 1586 and 13 injuries. Türkiye recorded more than 55,000 evacuations and 17 deaths during 1587 multiple western and northwestern fires, while Greece reported evacuations from at 1588 least 29 communities, 15,000 people evacuated, 17 firefighter injuries and one fatality. 1589 ● EFFIS reported that 39.3% of EU27 BA occurred within protected sites, the highest 1590 share on record, including 1,717 km<sup>2</sup> in Spain, 935 km<sup>2</sup> in Romania and 513 km<sup>2</sup> in 1591 Portugal. 1592 Beyond the insights captured by the regional panel report, we report on indicators of extreme 1593 fire behaviour within Europe. Our analyses show that although continental BA was below 1594 average, the Mediterranean fire season was exceptional in the individual-fire diagnostics, 1595 especially in Iberia: 1596 ● Peak fire radiative intensity across Europe was 57% above average and the highest in 1597 the record. 1598 ● The European Mediterranean forests, woodlands and scrub biome recorded its highest 1599 95th percentile fire size, highest 95th percentile rate of growth and highest peak fire 1600 radiative intensity. 1601 ● Spain showed the strongest national fire behaviour anomaly in Europe: 95th percentile 1602 fire size was 790% above average, rate of growth 504% above average and peak 1603 intensity 121% above average.
