- 1604 ● Portugal also had exceptional fire behaviour anomalies, with 95th percentile fire size 1605 266% above average, rate of growth 268% above average and peak intensity 165% 1606 above average. 1607 ● Albania recorded its highest 95th percentile fire size and rate of growth, at 349% and 1608 225% above average, respectively. 1609 ● The United Kingdom recorded its highest number of mapped fires, 234% above 1610 average, and its second-highest 95th percentile fire size, 146% above average. 1611 ● Within Spain, Galicia and Castilla y León had especially extreme individual-fire metrics, 1612 with 95th percentile fire size more than 10 times the average in both regions. 1613 1614 **3.3.4. North America** 

1615 The 2025-2026 fire season in North America marked a third consecutive year of extreme 1616 wildfire activity, driven primarily by extensive BA and emissions in Canada. Based on the 1617 MODIS BA and GFED fire carbon emissions analyses (Section 2.1.2), approximately 90,725 1618 km<sup>2</sup> burned across the continent between March 2025 and February 2026, ranking 8th in the 1619 24-year BA record, while fire carbon emissions reached 417.4 TgC, 184% of the 20-year 1620 average and the third highest total since 2003. Canada experienced its third most severe fire 1621 season on record for BA, with more than 73,000 km<sup>2</sup> burned according to national fire 1622 agency reporting, and its second-highest fire carbon emissions on record after 2023-2024 in 1623 the fire carbon emissions record. Fire activity was especially anomalous in central Canada: 1624 Manitoba, Saskatchewan, the Northwest Territories and Ontario all ranked among their top 1625 five fire seasons, with absolute BA anomalies exceeding +8,000 km<sup>2</sup> in both Manitoba and 1626 Saskatchewan. At the national scale, Mexico and Cuba were also among the largest positive 1627 relative anomalies for fire carbon emissions in North America. The key events of the 20251628 2026 fire season are summarised by Kolden et al. (2026) and included: 1629 ● Anomalous BA and fire carbon emissions were concentrated in central boreal Canada, 1630 especially Manitoba and Saskatchewan, rather than in the western and eastern 1631 Canadian regions associated with recent record fire seasons. 1632 ● Manitoba recorded its highest BA on record following an early heatwave, including 1633 37°C in Winnipeg on 13 May, while Saskatchewan also experienced extensive boreal 1634 burning. 1635 ● Canadian fire carbon emissions were dominated by central provinces in the GFED 1636 record: Manitoba emitted 79.9 TgC, 11.7 times its 2003-2026 average, while 1637 Saskatchewan emitted 116.9 TgC, 477% of normal. 1638 ● Ontario and New Brunswick also recorded their highest fire carbon emissions in the 1639 2003-2026 GFED record, while New Brunswick and Nova Scotia had above-average 1640 BA, including unusually late season fire activity. 1641 ● Major societal impacts occurred in central Canada, with statistics from agency and 1642 media reporting including two civilian deaths, more than 31,000 evacuations in 1643 Manitoba, more than 26,000 in Saskatchewan, and hundreds of homes destroyed. 1644 Many of the evacuated communities were remote Indigenous First Nations 1645 communities, and fire response was challenged by limited suppression capacity in 1646 provinces that historically experience fewer large fires than western Canada. 1647 ● Mexico recorded over 12,300 km<sup>2</sup> burned according to the Comisión Nacional Forestal 1648 (CONAFOR) database, its second-highest total since records began in 1970; this was 1649 385% of the 1970-2024 long term average and 249% of the 2005-2024 average. 1650 ● The United States recorded near average BA according to U.S National Interagency 1651 Fire Center (NIFC) reporting, with more than 21,500 km<sup>2</sup> burned, but 77,850 fires were 1652 detected, the highest number since 2011 and 25% above the previous decade’s 1653 average. 1654 ● In March 2025, NIFC reported that grassland fires in Oklahoma and the Texas 1655 Panhandle burned more than 688 km<sup>2</sup> , destroyed over 500 structures, injured more 1656 than 200 people, and killed four people.

- 1657 ● Several US fires were notable for unusual timing or conditions, including North and 1658 South Carolina fires partly fuelled by Hurricane Helene debris, the Minnesota Camp 1659 House Fire that destroyed 187 structures, and mid winter fires in Colorado and the 1660 Great Plains. 1661 ● The Dragon Bravo Fire in Arizona burned 588 km<sup>2</sup> in and around Grand Canyon 1662 National Park and destroyed 113 structures, including the historic Grand Canyon 1663 Lodge. 

1664 Beyond the insights captured by the regional panel report, we report on indicators of extreme 1665 fire behaviour within North America. Our analyses show that the strong fire carbon emissions 1666 anomaly was accompanied by record continental fire intensity and widespread fire 1667 occurrence, especially across central Canada: 

- 1668 ● North America recorded its highest peak fire radiative intensity in the record, 61% 1669 above average. 1670 ● Continental 95th percentile fire size and 95th percentile rate of growth were also 1671 elevated, at 77% and 31% above average. 1672 ● The North American boreal forest had a record number of mapped fires, 370% above 1673 average, but 95th percentile fire size and rate of growth were below average. 1674 ● Canada recorded its highest number of mapped fires, 339% above average, and its 1675 second-highest peak fire radiative intensity, 31% above average. 1676 ● Manitoba recorded its highest number of mapped fires, 541% above average, 1677 alongside elevated fire size and peak intensity. 1678 ● Saskatchewan also recorded its highest number of mapped fires, 624% above 1679 average, and high peak intensity, but 95th percentile fire size and rate of growth were 1680 below average. 1681 ● Ontario showed positive anomalies across fire count, 95th percentile fire size, rate of 1682 growth and peak intensity, reinforcing its high-ranking BA and emissions season. 1683 1684 **3.3.5. Oceania** 1685 

1686 The 2025-26 fire season in Australia was notable for the high number of fires rather than 1687 exceptional total BA. Approximately 477,000 km<sup>2</sup> burned between March 2025 and February 1688 2026, ranking 10th in the 24-year record, while the number of detected fires was the highest 1689 in the period of record. The key events of the 2025-2026 fire season were summarised by 1690 Reynolds et al. (2026) and included: 

1691 1692 ● Anomalous high BA extent was concentrated in northern savanna and arid-zone 1693 systems of Australia, where fuel availability was above usual due to antecedent rainfall 1694 in early 2025. In the calendar year 2025, 455,000 km<sup>2</sup> burned in Australia’s tropical 1695 savannas, the largest extent since 2012, including almost 300,000 km<sup>2</sup> in the Northern 1696 Territory (North Australia and Rangelands Fire Information, 2026). Some desert 1697 regions in Western Australia and the Northern Territory experienced more than twice 1698 the median annual burned extent of the previous 25 years. 1699 ● Several large fires occurred in sparsely populated savanna, rangeland and desert 1700 landscapes of Australia under continuous-fuel conditions. Notable examples included: 1701 the Tanami Desert fire complex (Northern Territory) alone burning >35,000 km<sup>2</sup> 1702 following dry lightning ignitions in fuels accumulated after successive wet years; a fire 1703 south of Broome (Western Australia) that burned >12,000 km<sup>2</sup> over 28 days; a 1704 Northern Territory fire that burned >7,000 km<sup>2</sup> over 62 days, and multiple Kimberley 1705 (Western Australia) and Queensland fires each exceeding 4,000 km<sup>2</sup> . 1706 ● The most severe societal impacts occurred in southern and eastern Australia, 1707 particularly Victoria and New South Wales. In Victoria, an early-January catastrophic 1708 fire-weather episode produced 25 major fires, approximately 4,000 km<sup>2</sup> burned, one 1709 fatality, over 450 homes destroyed, and the loss of around 45,000 livestock, following

- 1710 24 months of persistent dryness. New South Wales experienced a prolonged season 1711 with >11,000 bush and grass fires, >2,000 km<sup>2</sup> burned, 26 homes destroyed, and one 1712 agency firefighter fatality. 1713 ● The season affected multiple landscapes of high ecological, cultural or international 1714 conservation significance. Fire burned 62% of Judburra/Gregory National Park in the 1715 Northern Territory; approximately 1,710 km<sup>2</sup> in and adjacent to Fitzgerald River 1716 National Park (Western Australia), the core of a UNESCO Biosphere Reserve; 40% of 1717 Deep Creek National Park (South Australia); and two areas of Tongariro National Park 1718 (New Zealand), a dual World Heritage site. 1719 ● Several fires led to significant societal impacts despite their not growing to be large. In 1720 Tasmania, the Dolphin Sands fire burned only approximately 7 km<sup>2</sup> but destroyed 19 1721 homes. In New Zealand, a fire near Kaikōura destroyed 14 buildings having spread 1722 under downslope winds reaching 200 km h<sup>-1</sup> . 1723 1724 Beyond the insights captured by the regional panel report, we report on indicators of extreme 1725 fire behaviour within Oceania. Our analyses show that Australia’s 2025-2026 season was most 1726 clearly expressed through widespread fire occurrence, particularly in savanna and arid-zone 1727 systems, rather than through exceptional national fire intensity: 1728 1729 ● Australia recorded its highest number of mapped fires in the record, 88% above 1730 average. 1731 ● National 95th percentile fire size and rate of growth were moderately elevated, at 21% 1732 and 22% above average. 1733 ● Peak fire radiative intensity in Australia was 8% below average, consistent with near1734 average or below-average national fire carbon emissions. 1735 ● The Australian (sub)tropical grassland, savannah and shrubland biome recorded its 1736 highest number of mapped fires, 105% above average. 1737 ● Australian deserts and xeric shrublands showed the clearest individual-fire anomaly, 1738 with fire count 124% above average, 95th percentile fire size 91% above average and 1739 rate of growth 59% above average. 1740 ● Victoria’s societal impacts were not matched by extreme state-wide fire size or growth 1741 diagnostics; peak intensity was close to average. 1742 

### 1743 **3.3.6. South America** 

1744 The 2025-2026 fire season in South America was notable for damaging localised fires in Chile 1745 and Argentina, major protected-area burning in Brazil, and increased fire activity in parts of 1746 the Caatinga, Pampa and Ceará, rather than exceptional continental BA. Based on the 1747 regional MODIS BA and GFED fire carbon emissions analyses, continental BA was the lowest 1748 since 2002, at 185,075 km<sup>2</sup> , and fire carbon emissions were below their post-2003 average. 1749 This low continental total occurred despite up to 30 additional extreme fire-weather days, 1750 relative to 2002-2025 conditions, across parts of the central continent, eastern coast and 1751 Chilean Pacific coast. The key events of the 2025-2026 fire season are summarised by 1752 Anderson et al. (2026) and included: 

- 1753 ● In Chile, the January 2026 Penco-Lirquén fire in Biobío burned approximately 200 1754 km<sup>2</sup> , caused 23 fatalities, destroyed thousands of houses and displaced 52,000 1755 evacuees to 11 shelters; the wider Chile-Argentina event is treated in detail in 1756 Section 6.1. 1757 ● In northwest Argentina, January 2026 fires affected the Andean forest belt and areas 1758 around Los Alerces National Park, a UNESCO World Heritage site, with reported 1759 damage exceeding approximately 445.5 km<sup>2</sup> by late January 2026 (see Section 6.1). 1760 ● The Brazilian National Institute for Space Research (INPE) recorded 3,224 active fire 1761 detections across Argentina’s northern provinces and Paraná basin floodplain from 1762 23-29 August 2025, including 1,074 hotspots on 29 August; Chubut recorded 529 

44

- 1763 AQUA/MODIS active fires across 21 days in January 2026, 390% more than in the 1764 previous year. 1765 ● Brazil recorded 136,393 active fires in INPE monitoring, 51% fewer than in 2024, the 1766 seventh-lowest annual total since INPE monitoring began in 1998 and 37.1% below 1767 the 1998-2024 average. 1768 ● Within Brazil, INPE active-fire counts were approximately 96% below the 1998-2024 1769 average in the Pantanal and 78% below average in the Amazon, but 26% above 1770 average in the Caatinga and 72% above average in the Pampa. 1771 ● MapBiomas reported that the Cerrado and Caatinga accounted for 71% of Brazil’s 1772 total BA in 2025, despite Brazil’s low national active-fire total in the INPE record. 1773 ● The Chapada dos Veadeiros National Park fire in central Goiás burned for more than 1774 21 days and affected at least 1,110 km<sup>2</sup> , equivalent to 46% of the park, and also 1775 reached the Avá-Canoeiro Indigenous Territory and the Kalunga Quilombola 1776 Territory. 1777 ● Ceará was a localised Brazilian exception in the regional analysis, with fire numbers 1778 334% above the long-term average and BA 35% above average, the highest fire1779 count anomaly and only positive BA anomaly among Brazilian states. 1780 ● Bolivia recorded a much lower fire season than in 2024, with INPE reporting 18,608 1781 active fire detections between February 2025 and March 2026 and national 1782 monitoring reporting 7,410 km<sup>2</sup> burned in 2025, 79% lower than the 124,000 km<sup>2</sup> 1783 burned in 2024. 1784 Beyond the insights captured by the regional panel report, we report on indicators of extreme 1785 fire behaviour within South America. Our analyses show that the continent’s low BA and fire 1786 carbon emissions totals masked some localised fire behaviour anomalies, but the strongest 1787 spreadsheet evidence remains concentrated in BA and emissions rather than consistently in 1788 the individual-fire diagnostics: 1789 ● South America had low BA and fire carbon emissions overall, yet 95th percentile fire 1790 size and rate of growth were above average, at 27% and 31%, respectively. Peak fire 1791 radiative intensity across South America was slightly below average, at -7%. 1792 ● The South American temperate broadleaf and mixed forest biome had elevated peak 1793 fire radiative intensity, 62% above average, supporting the localised southern 1794 temperate forest anomaly. 1795 ● Chubut’s strong BA and fire carbon emissions anomalies were not matched by an 1796 equally clear peak-intensity anomaly in the fire behaviour diagnostics. 1797 ● Brazil was below average nationally for BA and fire carbon emissions, but fires that did 1798 occur were relatively large and fast-growing, with 95th percentile fire size 71% above 1799 average and rate of growth 62% above average. 1800 ● Piauí was a localised Brazilian exception, with fire count 135% above average, 95th 1801 percentile fire size 68% above average and rate of growth 42% above average. 1802 1803 **3.3.7. Focal Regions** 1804 1805 For this report, we select three focal regions that experienced significant impacts and 1806 anomalously high BA and emissions (see Section 2.3). In each case we analyse the nature 1807 and impact of the event, the contributing hydroclimatic drivers, and attribution to climate 1808 change today and in future projections. Our focal regions and event periods are: Midwestern 1809 Canadian Shield Forests (July-August 2025); the Northwest Iberian Peninsula (which we refer 1810 to as Northwest Iberia) (August 2025); the Chilean Temperate Forests and Matorral (January1811 February 2026). 1812 1813 1814 1815 1816

1817 **4. Focal Region 1: Midwestern Canadian Shield Forests** 1818 1819 **4.1. Nature and Impact of the Event** 1820 Midwestern Canadian Shield Forests were selected as a focal region because the 2025 fires 1821 formed the central Canadian core of a third consecutive extreme wildfire season in Canada 1822 (Chen et al., 2026; Tymstra and Flannigan, 2025; Jones et al., 2026a), with exceptional BA 1823 and fire carbon emissions concentrated across the boreal forest regions of Saskatchewan, 1824 Manitoba, and Ontario (Figure 7). The fires developed from mid-May and continued through 1825 summer. By the end of 2025, Saskatchewan had burned almost 23,000 km<sup>2</sup> , Manitoba over 1826 19,000 km<sup>2</sup> , and Ontario almost 5,000 km<sup>2</sup> , together accounting for more than half of Canada’s 1827 BA in 2025 (Canadian Interagency Forest Fire Centre, 2025; Skakun et al., 2024; Section 1828 3.3.4). While many of these fires were large in size, consistent with boreal forest fires in 1829 Canada, they were also unusually long duration fires, with many igniting or flaring up in mid1830 May and burning well into September and October, for a much longer than normal fire season. 1831 Consistent with large wildfire years in the boreal forests of Canada, the focal region 1832 experienced a prolonged heat wave in early May 2025 that produced substantial drying across 1833 the central portion of the Canadian shield. This built on a relatively dry winter and facilitated 1834 high ignition rates both from new lightning strikes across the region and an unusually high 1835 number of overwintering fires that had smoldered in the duff since the 2024 fire season 1836 (Saskatchewan Public Safety Agency, 2025), a phenomenon that has been increasing in 1837 frequency across Canada and boreal forests globally (Scholten et al. 2021). While provincial 1838 wildfire services in Canada generally have limited ability to suppress wildfires in remote areas 1839 and instead focus on protection of critical resources and communities, the 2025 fire season 1840 impacted so many remote communities (particularly First Nations Indigenous communities) in 1841 Saskatchewan, Manitoba, and northern Ontario that it strained provincial emergency response 1842 capability substantially. The Canadian Armed Forces supported evacuations and logistics in 1843 Manitoba and Ontario, while firefighters were deployed to Canada from the United States, 1844 Australia, New Zealand, Costa Rica and Mexico including about 700 firefighters from the 1845 United States Forest Service in August 2025. 1846 By May 29, Saskatchewan had already declared a provincial emergency (Saskatchewan 1847 Public Safety Agency, 2025), while the Canadian Interagency Forest Fire Centre had already 1848 raised the Preparedness Level to 5 (the maximum), signalling that all provincial and territorial 1849 firefighting resources were in use and that additional resources were also needed. Further, 1850 these fires exhibited extreme behavior that produced tragic consequences when two civilians 1851 were killed in Manitoba on May 13 near Lac du Bonnet, northeast of Winnipeg. By early June, 1852 over 25,000 people had already been forced to evacuate, and a second wave of fires in July 1853 produced additional evacuations across the region. Public Safety Canada reported that over 1854 70,000 people were evacuated from 116 communities in 2025 across the country, with 64% 1855 of evacuees coming from 72 First Nations communities (Public Safety Canada, 2025). These 1856 evacuations were prolonged for many communities (weeks-to-months), and as most were in 1857 the Midwestern Canada boreal forest fires, the limited availability of housing and hotel rooms 1858 available for evacuees forced many to neighboring provinces hundred of kilometers away 1859 (Province of Manitoba, 2026). 1860 The regional event had substantial economic, infrastructure and smoke-related impacts. 1861 Insured wildfire losses in Manitoba and Saskatchewan approached CAD 300 million 1862 (approximately USD 215 million), including CAD 249 million (approximately USD 180 million) 1863 around Flin Flon, Manitoba (The Insurer, 2025). A media analysis estimated more than CAD 1864 500 million (approximately USD 360 million) in direct expenses, including insured damages, 1865 evacuations, emergency costs and health-related costs (The Narwhal, 2025). Among the most 1866 culturally significant buildings destroyed was the Robertson Trading Post near La Ronge,

The focal<br>region lies within carbon-rich boreal forest ecosystems where large, high-intensity fires are a<br>natural disturbance process, but where repeated extreme fire years can increase the<br>proportion of landscape in recently burned or degraded states and weaken post-fire vegetation<br>and carbon recovery (Jones et al., 2026a; Baltzer et al., 2021). Around 70% of Canada’s<br>exceptional boreal forest fire emissions in 2025 came from the Midwestern Shield and Mid-<br>Continental forests of Saskatchewan, Manitoba and Ontario, where emissions were around<br>seven times the average (Jones et al., 2026a; Section 3.1).|
|1898<br>1899<br>1900<br>1901<br>1902<br>1903<br>1904<br>1905|The wildfire smoke emissions generated substantial aerosol optical depth (AOD) increases<br>over central Canada, with smoke transported across eastern Canada, eastern US and the<br>North Atlantic. Weather was altered regionally by the smoke, with strong surface cooling<br>across eastern Canada and eastern US: modelling work by Roşu et al. (2026) indicates<br>average temperature anomalies of -0.7°C and local anomalies as great as -1.7 °C.|

|1933<br>1934<br>1935<br>1936|The large Midwest Canadian Shield Forest experienced sustained below-average<br>precipitation during the months preceding the peak fire activity, with precipitation anomalies<br>remaining consistently negative up to 4 months before the start of the main event (Figure 8a).<br>This prolonged lack of rainfall progressively depleted both surface and subsurface moisture|
|1937<br>1938|reservoirs, producing severe groundwater depletion and critically low deep-soil moisture<br>conditions. The persistence of these deficits meant that vegetation entered the fire season|
|1939<br>1940<br>1941<br>1942<br>1943|under long-term hydrological stress rather than only transient meteorological drought. As a<br>consequence, both live and dead fuels became exceptionally dry and highly combustible over<br>extensive areas, with fuel moisture conditions reaching among the driest portions of the<br>historical record (z-score>2.5 corresponding to 99th of the record) and indicating an extreme<br>level of landscape-scale flammability.|
|1944<br>1945<br>1946<br>1947<br>1948<br>1949<br>1950<br>1951<br>1952<br>1953|During the peak burning periods, repeated episodes of anomalously warm and dry<br>atmospheric conditions further intensified fire danger by accelerating evapotranspiration and<br>fuel curing. These episodes substantially accelerated the<br>desiccation of already stressed fuels and favoured rapid fire growth. Strong wind anomalies<br>also contributed to fire spread and extreme fire behaviour, with several episodes associated<br>with wind conditions stronger than usual enhancing oxygen supply and promoting long-range<br>spotting processes. In Canada, wind acted as an amplifying factor superimposed on an<br>already dry landscape.|
|1954<br>1955<br>1956<br>1957<br>1958<br>1959<br>1960<br>1961<br>1962<br>1963<br>1964<br>1965|Our formal causality analysis further confirms the dominant role of hydroclimatic and fuel<br>drivers during the 2025 event relative to previous fire seasons (Figure 8b,c). Fuel moisture variables also<br>showed exceptionally strong positive contributions, with live fuel moisture and dead fuel<br>moisture contributing around 22% and 14%, respectively, highlighting the importance of<br>prolonged vegetation desiccation and critically dry fuel conditions. In contrast, variables<br>associated with ignition processes and static landscape characteristics contributed relatively<br>little to the overall anomaly when aggregated over the full period. Human presence and road<br>network nonetheless contributed positively (around  7% and 2%, respectively), indicating that<br>background exposure and ecosystem characteristics modulated the final extent of the event.|

|2174|**5.1.**<br>**Nature and Impact of the Event**|
|2175<br>2176<br>2177<br>2178<br>2179|Northwest Iberia was selected as a focal region because it was the principal centre of Europe’s<br>record-breaking 2025 wildfire season (**Section 3.3.3**). For a closely matching Northwest Iberia<br>study region, though not precisely identical to the focal region used here, Sánchez-Hernández<br>et al. (2025) estimated that more than half of the total area burned across Europe in 2025 was<br>in Northwest Iberia.|
|2180<br>2181<br>2182<br>2183<br>2184<br>2185<br>2186<br>2187<br>2188|The fires developed primarily over July and August, but mostly from 8 to 26 August, and<br>included multiple large fires burning concurrently across central and northern Portugal and<br>northwest Spain (Keeping et al., 2025; Sánchez-Hernández et al., 2025). Satellite-derived fire<br>mapping shows that 14 of these fires exceeded 100 km<sup>2</sup>, meeting the commonly used<br>threshold for megafires (Sánchez-Hernández et al., 2025). August 2025 was also the highest<br>BA month in the closely matching Northwest Iberia region during the 1985-2025 record,<br>exceeding other major fire events including October 2017, when destructive fires affected<br>Portugal and Galicia (Spain) during the exceptional wind conditions associated with Hurricane<br>Ophelia (Sánchez-Hernández et al., 2025; Ramos et al., 2023).|
|2189<br>2190<br>2191<br>2192<br>2193<br>2194<br>2195<br>2196|The focal region experienced repeated large, long-duration and resource-intensive fires<br>spreading over mountainous terrain predominantly occupied by shrubland, as summarised by<br>Fernandes et al. (2026). Spain recorded its largest and third largest wildfires ever (Sedano et<br>al., 2026): the Molezuelas de la Carballeda fire in Zamora (Castilla y León), attaining 401 km<sup>2</sup>,<br>and the Larouco wildfire that burned 372 km<sup>2</sup>in Ourense (Galicia). In central Portugal, the<br>Arganil fire lasted for 13 days and burned 654 km<sup>2</sup>, being the largest on record. Further north,<br>the Trancoso complex grew to 560 km<sup>2</sup>after 16 days, and maintained an average rate of<br>spread of 3.2 kmh<sup>-1</sup>for 8 hours on August 14 (Comissão Técnica Independente, 2026).|
|2197<br>2198<br>2199<br>2200<br>2201<br>2202<br>2203|Nine of the 14 fatalities and more than 90% of the evacuations across Spain and Portugal,<br>(around 40,000 people), occurred in this region (Agência para a Gestão Integrada de Fogos<br>Rurais, 2026; Dirección General de Protección Civil y Emergencias, 2026). Additional societal<br>impacts were substantial, namely hindered circulation, poor air and water quality, power<br>outages, and loss of agricultural production (Fernandes et al., 2026). Both countries activated<br>the EU Civil Protection Mechanism for wildfire assistance, underlining the pressure placed on<br>national and shared European emergency-response capacity.|
|2204<br>2205<br>2206<br>2207<br>2208<br>2209<br>2210|The event also had substantial ecological and landscape impacts. Sánchez-Hernández et al.<br>(2025) estimated that around 32% of the BA lay within protected areas and Quintero et al.<br>(2026) estimated that 44% of BA lay within Europe’s_Natura 2000_sites, though no evidence<br>was found for a difference in the severity of fires between those sites. Protected natural areas<br>affected within or overlapping the focal region included the Peneda-Gerês National Park<br>(Portugal) and Picos de Europa National Park (Keeping et al., 2025). Areas of high ecological<br>and cultural value were exposed. Habitat for endangered, vulnerable, or specially protected|
|2211<br>2212<br>2213|<br>species was damaged, including areas identified as critical for capercaillie and brown bear<br>(Keeping et al., 2025). The Las Médulas UNESCO World Heritage and significant stretches of<br>the Camino de Santiago pilgrimage routes, which pass through the northwest Spanish part of|
|2214<br>2215|<br>the focal region and typically attract large numbers of summer visitors, were also affected<br>(Keeping et al., 2025).|

2228 **Figure 12: Hydroclimatic evolution and causal analysis of the focal Iberia fire event.** (a) 2229 Standardised hydroclimatic anomalies (z-scores) from 120 days before to 30 days after the 2230 onset of the main fire event, showing the temporal evolution of key atmospheric and fuel-

|2231<br>|related drivers relative to the 2003–2024 climatology. The main focal event is highlighted and<br>|
|---|---|
|2232<br>2233<br>2234<br>2235<br>2236<br>2237<br>2238<br>2239<br>2240|represents the period over which the drivers are attributed in panel (b) as anomaly compared<br>to the 2003-2024 baseline. (c) Grouped attribution analysis of BA anomalies for all fire seasons<br>from 2003 to the present, showing the relative contribution of fuel-related (Fuel, orange),<br>weather-related (Weather, green), and ignition/suppression-related (Ign-Supp, pink) drivers to<br>interannual fire variability. Years are ranked according to the observed BA over the reporting<br>year, defined from March to February. Note that this panel (c) compares fire activity over the<br>full reporting year, while panel (b) focuses on the main active period to provide a more event-<br>specific attribution.|
|2241<br>2242<br>2243<br>2244<br>2245<br>2246<br>2247<br>2248<br>2249|According to our analysis (Figure 12a), the extreme fire activity during the 2025 fire season in<br>Northwest Iberia was strongly shaped by a hydroclimatic rebound in which an anomalously<br>productive spring was followed by persistent summer drying and repeated heat extremes. In<br>contrast to fire seasons driven solely by short-term weather anomalies, the landscape in<br>Northwest Iberia became increasingly fire-prone several months in advance through the<br>accumulation of abundant and highly connected fuels. Wet conditions during late April and<br>early May, with precipitation anomalies locally exceeding +2 standard deviations, promoted<br>vegetation growth and fuel build-up across the region. This antecedent productivity created a<br>large reservoir of biomass available for burning later in the season.|
|2250<br>2251<br>2252<br>2253<br>2254<br>2255<br>2256<br>2257<br>2258|From late May onward, the region transitioned into a prolonged period of precipitation deficits<br>and progressive drying. Rainfall anomalies remained persistently negative through most of<br>June, July, and August, while temperatures became increasingly anomalous. The first major<br>heatwave developed at the end of June and beginning of July, when temperature anomalies<br>exceeded +2 standard deviations over several consecutive days. A second intense warm<br>episode occurred in late July. These periods were accompanied by strongly negative relative<br>humidity anomalies, indicating exceptionally dry atmospheric conditions conducive to rapid<br>fuel desiccation and fire spread. The combined effect of sustained meteorological drought and<br>repeated heat extremes progressively made the landscape highly flammable.|
|2259<br>2260<br>2261<br>2262<br>2263|This sequence of anomalously wet and productive conditions followed by hot and dry weather<br>is consistent with the hydroclimatic rebound mechanism identified in other fire-prone<br>Mediterranean ecosystems, where enhanced vegetation growth during wet phases<br>subsequently amplifies fire risk once drought and heatwave conditions develop (McNorton et<br>al., 2025).|
|2264<br>2265|During the most intense fire period (10 to 19 August 2025), our analysis indicates that<br>concurrent fire-weather conditions were the dominant hydroclimatic driver of BA anomalies  in|
|2266<br>2267<br>2268<br>2269<br>2270<br>2271|<br>Northwest Iberia. Temperature and relative humidity anomalies accounted for more than 70%<br>of the predictions (Figure 12b) and 110% of the positive anomaly relative to the climatological<br>baseline (Figure 12c), highlighting the overwhelming role of extreme atmospheric conditions<br>in driving the final BA. In contrast, wind speed contributed only marginally and slightly<br>negatively, suggesting that the severity of the fires was not primarily associated with<br>anomalously strong wind events but rather with exceptionally hot and dry conditions.|
|2272<br>2273<br>2274<br>2275<br>2276<br>2277<br>2278<br>2279<br>2280|Fuel variables also played an important enabling role. Live fuel load +8% (an increase of 12%<br>compared to 2003-2024 average, live fuel moisture +6% (and +9% compared to previous<br>years), dead fuel load +3% (+5%), and dead fuel moisture +4% (+6%) all contributed positively<br>to fire activity, consistent with the antecedent hydroclimatic rebound that promoted substantial<br>vegetation growth during spring before progressive summer drying rendered these fuels highly<br>flammable. In particular, the positive contribution from both live and dead fuel components<br>indicates that the landscape entered the fire season with an unusually large and continuous<br>biomass reservoir capable of sustaining extensive fire spread once atmospheric conditions<br>became favourable.|



60 

https://doi.org/10.5194/essd-2026-587 Preprint. Discussion started: 3 August 2026 _⃝_ c Author(s) 2026. CC BY 4.0 License. 





- 2281 Anomalies in ignition-related contributions to the prediction were limited, accounting for 2282 approximately 1% of the total attribution. Lightning activity exhibited a slightly negative 2283 contribution relative to climatology (−4%). This does not imply that lightning was unimportant, 2284 but rather that its relative contribution to the exceptional fire season was smaller than in 2285 previous years, with fuel and weather conditions playing a comparatively larger role. Static 2286 landscape and anthropogenic variables, including vegetation type, land cover, road networks, 2287 and human presence, remained close to their climatological conditions and therefore primarily 2288 acted as background preconditioning factors rather than direct drivers of the extreme event. 

- 2289 Overall, the hydroclimatic driver analysis highlights a compound mechanism in which 2290 antecedent fuel accumulation created the conditions for large fire growth, while exceptional 2291 heat and atmospheric dryness ultimately controlled the timing and severity of the extreme BA 2292 event.

|2379<br>2380<br>2381<br>2382<br>2383|2.33 (1.4 to 11.36), more than twice the BA for an event with a likelihood comparable to that<br>of August 2025. |2472|**6.1.**<br>**Nature and Impact of the Event**|
|2473<br>2474<br>2475<br>2476<br>2477<br>2478<br>2479<br>2480|Chile and northwest Argentina were selected as a focal region because the January 2026 fires<br>combined a deadly urban-interface disaster in south-central Chile with contemporaneous fires<br>in Andean temperate forests in south-central Chile and western Argentina. The selected region<br>is defined very similarly to the regions analysed by Barnes et al. (2026): a Chilean region<br>spanning the most affected areas around Ñuble, Biobío and La Araucanía, and a northern<br>Patagonian region across the Chile-Argentina border that included the areas around Los<br>Alerces and Lago Puelo National Parks. A detailed report on the impacts are presented in<br>Barnes et al. (2026) and a summary can be read in Anderson et al. (2026).|
|2481<br>2482<br>2483<br>2484<br>2485<br>2486<br>2487<br>2488<br>2489<br>2490<br>2491|The Chilean part of the focal region experienced substantial loss of life, displacement and<br>damage to the built environment, detailed on the report by Barnes et al., (2026) and as<br>summarised by Anderson et al. (2026). In the Biobío and Ñuble regions, fires started on 14<br>December and escalated to large-scale fires on 16 January, spreading rapidly to the south<br>and east, in the direction of  La Araucanía. On 17 January 2026, the most destructive activity<br>occurred near Concepción, where three nearby ignition points merged into the Penco-Lirquén<br>fire. This fire remained active for approximately 36–48 hours, burned about 200 km<sup>2</sup>, spread<br>towards the coastal settlements of Penco-Lirquén and Punta Parra, and caused 23 fatalities,<br>destroyed thousands of houses and damaged critical infrastructure, with 52,000 evacuations<br>displaced to 11 shelters (Barnes et al., 2026). It was declared a state of catastrophe by Chile’s<br>President on the 18<sup>th</sup>January 2026.|
|2492<br>2493<br>2494<br>2495<br>2496<br>2497<br>2498<br>2499<br>2500|In northwest Argentina, in the northernmost parts of Patagonia, fires affected temperate<br>forests, protected areas and regional tourism. Fires began on the 5<sup>th</sup>January, in the Puerto<br>Patriada area, during the height of the tourist season, when more than 3,000 visitors were<br>present. In one day, fires burned 18 km<sup>2</sup>, forcing the entire village to evacuate. On January<br>21, the government declared a state of emergency in the Argentine Patagonia region, with the<br>evacuation of at least 3,000 people, including tourists and residents from El Hoyo, Puerto<br>Patriada and Epuyén (ECHO, 2026). By early February, more than 450 km<sup>2</sup>of native and<br>planted forest, shrublands and grasslands burned, including an estimated burned loss of 75%<br>of Epuyén’s native forests (Barnes et al., 2026).|
|2501<br>2502|The event also had substantial ecological and landscape impacts. In Chile, the Penco-Lirquén<br>fire burned through a landscape dominated by non-native forest plantations, including_Pinus_|
|2503<br>2504<br>2505<br>2506<br>2507<br>2508<br>2509<br>2510<br>2511|<br>_radiata_and_Eucalyptus_species, adjacent to settlements (Barnes et al., 2026). In Argentina,<br>the fires affected native forest, shrublands and grasslands, with native forest reported as the<br>most affected vegetation type in Chubut wildfire records and with important consequences for<br>wildlife, soils and water resources (Secretaría de Bosques, 2025; Anderson et al., 2026,<br>Barnes et al., 2026). Barnes et al. (2026) emphasise that fires in Patagonia placed pressure<br>on endemic and vulnerable species, including huemul and pudú, as well the Patagonian black<br>woodpecker, and encroached on Los Alerces National Park, a UNESCO World Heritage Site<br>known for ancient alerce trees.|

(c) Grouped attribution analysis of BA anomalies for all fire seasons<br>from 2003 to the present, showing the relative contribution of fuel-related (Fuel, orange),<br>weather-related (Weather, green), and ignition/suppression-related (Ign-Supp, pink) drivers to<br>interannual fire variability. Years are ranked according to the observed BA over the reporting<br>year, defined from March to February. Note that this panel (c) compares fire activity over the<br>full reporting year, while panel (b) focuses on the main active period to provide a more event-<br>specific attribution.|
|2551<br>2552<br>2553<br>2554<br>2555<br>2556<br>2557<br>2558<br>2559<br>2560<br>2561|The hydroclimatic evolution of the 2026 Chile fire event was characterised by a prolonged<br>period of anomalously dry conditions followed by the emergence of extreme wind conditions<br>during the peak burning phase (Figure 16a). Although daily precipitation anomalies were<br>predominantly negative during the months preceding the event, precipitation is inherently<br>intermittent and daily z-scores are less informative than integrated hydrological indicators. The<br>cumulative impact of the rainfall deficit is therefore better reflected by the persistent negative<br>anomalies in soil moisture, vegetation condition, and fuel moisture, indicating sustained<br>moisture deficits and limited hydrological recovery across the landscape. These prolonged dry<br>conditions likely contributed to progressive fuel desiccation and the depletion of ecosystem<br>water reserves, particularly in deep-layer soil moisture, well before the onset of extreme fire<br>activity.|
|2562<br>2563<br>2564<br>2565<br>2566<br>2567<br>2568<br>2569|The evolution of fuel moisture further supports this interpretation. Both live and dead fuel<br>moisture exhibited persistent negative anomalies throughout the preconditioning phase,<br>reflecting widespread vegetation stress and critically dry fine fuels prior to ignition and rapid<br>fire spread. These conditions are consistent with prolonged hydroclimatic drying and reduced<br>soil moisture availability, which together weakened ecosystem moisture resilience and<br>increased landscape flammability. The persistence of these fuel-moisture anomalies indicates<br>that the extreme fire event was not driven solely by short-term fire weather, but by a cumulative<br>drying process that developed over several weeks to months.|
|2570<br>2571<br>2572<br>2573<br>2574<br>2575<br>2576<br>2577|During the central phase of the event (around the 10<sup>th</sup>of January), atmospheric dynamics<br>became increasingly important. Wind speed anomalies intensified sharply around the peak<br>burning period, locally exceeding +2 to +3 standard deviations, indicating exceptionally strong<br>and anomalous wind conditions compared to climatology. These episodic wind extremes likely<br>played a critical role in accelerating fire spread, enhancing spotting processes, and increasing<br>fire-line intensity once fuels had already reached critically dry states. The combination of<br>antecedent drying and extreme winds was the primary mechanism controlling the extent of<br>the Chile event.|
|2578<br>2579<br>2580<br>2581<br>2582<br>2583<br>2584<br>2585<br>2586|The hydroclimatic driver analysis for the central phase of the Chile event reveals an<br>exceptionally strong compound influence of both fire weather and fuel-related drivers relative<br>to all previous years in the record (Figure 16b). Weather-related factors accounted for<br>approximately 40% of the anomalous BA prediction, while fuel-related contributions exceeded<br>53%, making this particular event one of the most strongly fuel-driven fire seasons in the entire<br>time series (Figure 16c). In contrast, ignition and suppression related variables contributed<br>only weakly (~2%), indicating that the extreme fire activity cannot primarily be explained by<br>anomalous ignition occurrence or human activity, but instead resulted from the interaction<br>between highly conducive environmental conditions and unusually flammable landscapes.|
|2587<br>2588<br>2589<br>2590<br>|Looking at the fire season as a whole and comparing it with previous fire years (Figure 16c),<br>the 2025-2026 season does not stand out as exceptionally fuel-driven, with weather-related<br>contributions remaining largely positive and fuel-related contributions only slightly negative.<br>This reflects the fact that the annual attribution integrates the entire fire season rather than<br>|
|2591|focusing on the peak burning period. Nevertheless, only a few historical seasons, notably|



70 

https://doi.org/10.5194/essd-2026-587 Preprint. Discussion started: 3 August 2026 _⃝_ c Author(s) 2026. CC BY 4.0 License. 





- 2592 2020, 2023, and 2024, showed comparable fuel-related signals, indicating that antecedent 2593 hydroclimatic conditions still played an important role in shaping the season. 

- 2594 These results suggest that the 2026 event emerged from a compound hydroclimatic 2595 configuration in which prolonged antecedent drying and fuel accumulation created highly 2596 vulnerable conditions that were subsequently amplified by severe atmospheric fire weather 2597 during the active burning phase. More broadly, this event highlights the importance of 2598 considering both slow hydrological processes and concurrent atmospheric extremes when 2599 interpreting the drivers of catastrophic wildfire events.

|2678|**6.3.2.**<br>**Changes in BA**|
|2679<br>2680<br>2681<br>2682<br>2683<br>2684|During January-February 2026, anthropogenic climate forcing very likely (92% likelihood)<br>increased BA across the Chilean Temperate Forests and Matorral region (Figure 18, Table 8).<br>BA was 1.36 (0.87 to 7.37) times larger under present-day conditions than in a counterfactual<br>climate. The increase was driven primarily by dry conditions, which were very likely to have<br>enhanced BA and produced an AF of 1.35 (1.00 to 12.93) (Table S13), in line with results from<br>the FWI analysis.|
|2685<br>2686<br>2687<br>2688<br>2689<br>2690|The largest increases were concentrated in the central part of the region, where BA was<br>amplified by a factor of two with high likelihood. In contrast, parts of the Andean-Patagonian<br>forests in Argentina exhibited decreases in BA under anthropogenic forcing. The most<br>severely affected locations, represented by the top 5% of BA grid cells, also experienced<br>increased BA (Figure S23, Table S14), with an AF of 1.2 (0.96 to 44.13), although confidence<br>in this signal was lower at 86% likelihood than for the region as a whole .|
|2691<br>2692<br>2693<br>2694<br>2695<br>2696<br>2697<br>2698<br>2699<br>2700<br>2701<br>2702|Future changes in BA across the Chilean Temperate Forests and Matorral become significant<br>(>90% likelihood) starting in the 2070s. However, there is potential for substantial, though<br>uncertain, increases much earlier in the century, with some SSP3-7.0 projections suggesting<br>AFs of up to 1.48 by the 2030s. By the 2090s, SSP5-8.5 projects a very likely (98% likelihood)<br>increase in BA, with AF reaching 3.17 for events with a likelihood comparable to the January–<br>February 2026 event. This increase is driven primarily by changes in weather conditions.<br>Weather-related amplification is virtually certain (>99% likelihood), with a central estimate of<br>approximately 1.66 and values of up to 2.6 by the end of the century. In contrast, fuel<br>responses remain uncertain and appear to be the dominant source of uncertainty in the overall<br>BA response. Fuel-related amplification ranges from 0.94 (a ~6% reduction) to 1.16 (a 16%<br>increase), and is only as likely as not (41%) to contribute to increased burning, though this<br>range is large enough to have a substantial impact on the final BA (Table S13).|
|2703<br>2704<br>2705<br>2706<br>2707<br>2708<br>2709<br>2710<br>2711<br>2712<br>2713<br>2714<br>2715|Under SSP3-7.0, the projected increase is smaller but remains significant, with a very likely<br>(97%) increase in BA and an AF of 1.34 (1.05–2.64) by the 2090s. This corresponds to a<br>9.04% (2.47 to 17.44%) lower BA than projected under SSP5-8.5, suggesting that mitigation<br>efforts already implemented have reduced future fire risk. These benefits may emerge well<br>before the end of the century, with projected BA reductions of around 5.33% (0.03-12.99%)<br>by the 2040s relative to SSP5-8.5. However, substantially larger benefits remain achievable<br>through stronger mitigation. Compared with SSP3-7.0, SSP1-2.6 is associated with a likely<br>(66%) and much more limited increase in BA by the 2090s, with AF constrained to 1.03 (0.80–<br>1.36). Relative to SSP3-7.0, SSP1-2.6 leads to a virtually certain (>99%) reduction in BA of<br>around 24.1% (18.57 to 48.54%) by 2090-2099. Benefits also emerge in the near term, with<br>virtually certain reductions relative to SSP3-7.0 of 5.77% (4.46-12.64%) by 2030-2039 and<br>3.45% (3.09 - 5.61%) by 2040-2049.|

|2793|**7.**<br>**Conclusions: Summary of the State of Wildfires in 2025-2026**|
|---|---|
|2794||
|<br>2795<br>2796|**7.1.**<br>**Extreme Wildfires of 2025-2026**|
|2797<br>2798|Impacts from extreme fires over 2025-2026 include widespread human, ecological and<br>economic losses, deteriorated air quality, and burning of protected areas and forest carbon|
|2799<br>2800|<br>projects. These impacts were driven by a pattern of regionally extreme wildfire events rather<br>than widespread anomalously high BA globally.|
|2801<br>2802<br>2803<br>2804|While global BA was below average, in line with continued decreases in savannahs and<br>grasslands in the past two decades,  a variety of anomalous fire seasons and extreme<br>individual fires were consequential at regional scales. Within this globally subdued fire year,<br>several distinct regional extremes emerged. The clearest positive fire carbon emissions|
|2805<br>2806|<br>anomaly occurred in North America, where continental emissions reached 417.4 TgC, 184%<br>of the 20-year average and the third-highest total since 2003. Fire carbon emissions in Canada|
|2807<br>2808<br>|reached 315 TgC, 195% above average and the second-highest in the record. Fires in Canada<br>forced 70,000 evacuations, triggered the declaration of Canada’s maximum firefighting<br>|
|2809|preparedness level and reportedly caused direct losses of USD 360 million. Europe also faced|
|<br>2810<br>2811|,<br>exceptional fire activity in 2025-26, with regional EFFIS statistics showing the largest BA total<br>on record (301% above its 2006-2024 average). Spain’s 95th-percentile fire size and rate of|
|2812<br>2813<br>2814<br>2815<br>|<br>growth were also 790% and 504% above average, respectively, while a fire complex in<br>Scotland became the UK’s first recorded megafire. In Australia, the number of mapped fires<br>was 88% above average and the highest in the record, although national BA and fire carbon<br>emissions totals remained close to or below average. In Asia, record or near-record BA<br>|
|2816<br>2817<br>2818|occurred in several countries, but South Korea was the clearest outlier: BA was 896% above<br>average and fires caused 32 fatalities and triggered the evacuation of approximately 37,000<br>people.|
|2819<br>2820<br>2821|These regional extremes were accompanied by contrasting patterns of human, economic and<br>ecological exposure. Africa recorded its lowest continental BA in the MODIS record, yet an<br>estimated 20.93 million people were within fire perimeters, 1.57 million more than average.|
|2822<br>2823|<br>Europe had US$4.11 billion in physical assets within fire perimeters, 2.2 times the average,<br>including US$2.05 billion in Greece. Fire-related PM2.5 produced above-average numbers of|
|2824<br>2825<br>2826|population-weighted poor air quality days in 45 countries; Albania experienced 10 additional<br>poor air quality days, while Norrbotten, Newfoundland and Labrador, and Saskatchewan<br>recorded 13, 12 and 10 exceedance days, respectively, despite having none under|
|2827<br>2828|<br>climatological fire emissions. Although the global extent of protected-area burning was the<br>lowest in the 24-year record, the percentage of national protected-area extent burned reached|
|2829<br>2830|record levels in South Korea, Cyprus, Bangladesh, the UK and Spain, at 56.1, 16.4, 6.2, 5.4<br>and 4.8 times their respective averages. Fire also affected 235 of the 1798 assessed forest|
|<br>2831<br>2832|,<br>carbon projects and burned 27,400km<sup>2</sup>within their boundaries; in Europe, project area burned<br>was 4.8 times the average.|
|2833<br>2834<br>2835|Although wildfire impacts were observed across many parts of the world, three focal regions<br>stand out as representative examples of the extreme fire activity seen during 2025–2026: the<br>Midwestern Canadian Shield Forests; Northwest Iberia; the Chilean Temperate Forests and|
|<br>2836|<br>Matorral. These regions experienced substantial societal and ecological impacts alongside|
|2837<br>2838<br>2839|<br>anomalously high BA and emissions, making them a useful lens through which to understand<br>the drivers of extreme fire seasons.|
|<br>2840|BA and fire carbon emissions were among the highest on record in Canada for a third|
|2841|consecutive year impacting air quality in Canada the US and beyond. Fires were|
|<br>2842|,     ,<br>concentrated in Manitoba and Saskatchewan, where there were a total of two civilian deaths,|
|2843|<br>57,000 total evacuations, and fire carbon emissions anomalies were 11.7 and 4.77 times their|

https://doi.org/10.5194/essd-2026-587 Preprint. Discussion started: 3 August 2026 _⃝_ c Author(s) 2026. CC BY 4.0 License. 





|2844<br>|respective averages. These fires drove high carbon losses from forests and affected local<br>|
|---|---|
|2845|weather, reinforcing a positive fire-climate feedback. Indeed, climate change has doubled the|
|2846|<br>likelihood of the extreme fire weather conditions seen during the 2025-2026 fire season in the|
|2847<br>2848<br>|Midwestern Canadian Shield Forests, and has made its extreme BA 46 times larger and 61<br>times more likely.|
|2849||
|2850<br>|In Europe, Iberia experienced a total of 14 megafires over August 2025, causing 14 fatalities<br>|
|2851|and evacuations of over 40,000 people. Northwest Iberia experienced some of its largest fires|
|2852|on record, with BA 25% larger as a result of anthropogenic climate change. This extreme fire|
|2853|activity caused widespread ecological and landscape damage, impacting national parks and|
|2854<br>|protected species, and led to the EU Civil Protection Mechanism for wildfire assistance being<br>|
|2855|activated across several countries simultaneously, placing pressure on Europe’s shared|
|2856<br>2857|<br>emergency-response capacity. If warming continues under SSP3-7.0, we could see 78% more<br>BA for events like the 2025-2026 fire season in this region by the 2090s, with some projections|
|2858|within the same scenario suggesting increases of around sevenfold. Extreme fires could be|
|2859<br>|up to 8% more likely, indicating greater future pressure on response systems. Ongoing policies<br>|
|2860<br>|to reduce greenhouse gas emissions have likely already reduced this projected increase by<br>|
|2861|around 23%, and further progress towards the Paris Agreement goals could reduce it by|
|2862|<br>another 30 to 70% by the 2090s, with some substantial reductions (~8%) even in the next 20|
|2863|years.|
|2864||
|2865<br>|The area affected by the deadly fires in Chile was around 36% greater due to anthropogenic<br>|

|2880<br>|Across all focal regions, the likelihood of extreme fire weather like we saw in 2025-2026 has<br>|
|2881<br>|increased due to climate change, and is projected to continue increasing at the same rate with<br>°|

|2883|<br>fold increase in Iberia, and a 50% increase in Chile. This behaviour is consistent across focal|
|2884|regions despite differences in baseline fire regimes, suggesting a broadly consistent scaling|
|2885|of extreme fire weather probability in a warming world. We also find that continued warming|
|<br>2886|<br>drives increases the expected total BA for future extreme events similar to those of 2025-2026,|
|2887|<br>and individual fires that do occur are likely to be larger and more intense as a result of climate|

|2890<br>|by a further 5-36%, and help limit the growth of individual fire sizes. Therefore, while we are<br>|
|2891|already witnessing the effects of climate change on extreme fires around the world, concerted|
|2892<br>2893|<br>efforts to reduce emissions and limit further warming can still help mitigate the worst impacts.|
|2894|Across the three focal regions, a common pattern of hydroclimatic drivers emerges from our|
|2895<br>|analysis. The most severe fire activity did not arise solely from short-lived weather extremes,<br>|
|2896|but from the interaction between long-term hydroclimatic preconditioning and acute|
|2897<br>|<br>atmospheric fire-weather conditions. In all three regions, fire-prone conditions developed<br>|
|2898|progressively over weeks to months as persistent dry anomalies increased landscape|

https://doi.org/10.5194/essd-2026-587 Preprint. Discussion started: 3 August 2026 _⃝_ c Author(s) 2026. CC BY 4.0 License. 





|2899<br>|flammability. In Canada and Chile, prolonged precipitation deficits led to groundwater<br>|
|---|---|
|2900<br>2901|depletion and widespread fuel desiccation. In Northwest Iberia, an anomalously wet spring<br>promoted exceptional vegetation growth, which was subsequently transformed into abundant,|
|2902<br>2903|highly flammable fuel by drought and heat. Despite these differing pathways, all regions<br>entered the fire season with elevated fuel loads or aridity that greatly amplified the effects of|
|2904<br>|extreme fire weather. During the peak burning phases, atmospheric conditions became the<br>|
|2905|primary control on fire growth, although the dominant mechanisms varied regionally. Canada|
|2906<br>2907<br>2908|<br>experienced a combination of heat, wind, and lightning-driven ignitions; Iberia was<br>overwhelmingly influenced by repeated heatwaves and very low humidity; and Chile was<br>characterised by exceptionally strong wind events superimposed on already desiccated fuels.|
|2909<br>|Driver quantification analyses consistently show that fuel-related processes and atmospheric<br>|
|2910<br>2911<br>2912<br>2913<br>2914<br>2915<br>2916|fire weather acted together rather than independently, producing compound conditions that<br>exceeded those observed in most previous years. Collectively, these events highlight a<br>broader emerging pattern in extreme wildfire seasons: catastrophic fire activity is increasingly<br>associated with the cascading interaction of slow hydrological processes that precondition<br>fuels, and short-term extreme fire weather that determines when and how rapidly fires spread<br>(Richardson et al., 2022; Abatzoglou et al., 2021; Libonati et al., 2022).|
|2917<br>2918<br>2919<br>|Despite interannual variability, implying that not every year is expected to become a large fire<br>year, and decreases in BA driven by fuel fragmentation that continue to dominate the global<br>trend, a persistent influence of climate change on regional extremes is clearly noticeable. Over<br>|
|2920<br>|the last three years of the State of Wildfires reports, we have reported on exceptional fires in<br>|
|2921|carbon-rich forests and peatlands across western Canada, Amazonia, Pantanal, and the|
|2922|<br>Congo Basin, driven by prolonged hydroclimatic drying prior to extreme fire weather. In|
|2923|contrast, fuel-limited regions including Northwest Iberia and Los Angeles experienced a|
|2924|hydroclimatic rebound with enhanced vegetation growth after anomalously wet conditions|
|<br>2925<br>2926|,        ,<br>followed by rapid drying and extreme heat and drought. Both patterns are symptomatic of<br>already visible effects of climate change; longer and hotter dry seasons driving more fires in|
|2927<br>2928|<br>fuel-abundant regions, and warmer, wetter winters followed by hotter, drier summers<br>propelling abnormally intense fires in fire-adapted ecoregions. Overlaid on the signal of|
|2929|background warming large-scale modes of variability such as El Niño Southern Oscillation|
|<br>2930<br>2931<br>2932<br>2933|,<br>can drive significant weather and fire extremes (Chen et al., 2017). With a strengthening El<br>Niño over 2026 (World Meteorological Organization, 2026), we can expect further records to<br>be broken over the coming months (Di Giuseppe and Burton, 2026).|
|2934<br>2935<br>|**7.2.**<br>**Progress in the State of Wildfires Report**<br>|
|2936<br>2937|Now in its third year, the State of Wildfires Report aims to be as community-driven as possible<br>to capture the widest breadth of cutting-edge knowledge from across the fire sciences. This|
|2938<br>2939<br>2940<br>|<br>edition has seen improvements across all sections of the report, including BA measurement<br>and impacts analysis, drivers, attribution, and projections science.<br>|
|2941<br>2942|For the extreme identification section of the report, we have now moved to GFED5 emissions<br>which gives an improved estimate of total carbon emissions, including small fires and|
|2943|<br>agricultural fires, and updated emission factors. While the overall magnitude of emissions is|
|2944|higher than previous versions of GFED, the trends remain consistent and therefore we|
|2945|continue to report anomalies in these reports to understand year to year variations We also|
|<br>2946|.<br>update the source of FRP observations, and the calculations of carbon emission anomalies|
|2947<br>2948|<br>and peak fire intensity. New for the impacts section this year, we include an assessment of<br>BA  i Pttd A|
||exposure n roece reas.|
|2949||
|2950<br>|The drivers analysis of the focal case studies demonstrates the value of moving beyond<br>|
|2951|traditional fire-weather assessments to an integrated hydroclimatic framework that links|
|2952<br>|<br>antecedent water availability, fuel dynamics, and atmospheric conditions. Across all events, a<br>|
|2953|common feature was strong long-term fuel memory, whereby persistent precipitation deficits,|



80 

https://doi.org/10.5194/essd-2026-587 Preprint. Discussion started: 3 August 2026 _⃝_ c Author(s) 2026. CC BY 4.0 License. 





|2954<br>|soil moisture and groundwater depletion, vegetation stress, and periods of anomalous fuel<br>|
|---|---|
|2955<br>2956|accumulation preconditioned landscapes for extreme fire activity over weeks to months before<br>fire seasons began. This indicates that much of the predictive information needed to anticipate|
|2957|<br>extreme wildfire events is stored within the fuel system rather than the atmosphere alone,|
|2958|meaning that conventional fire danger indices may fail to capture the longer-term pathways|
|2959<br>|through which hydroclimatic anomalies influence fuel availability and flammability. Since the<br>|
|2960<br>|State of Wildfires project began we have already made significant progress in improving the<br>|
|2961<br>2962|representation of fuel and therefore the hydrological memory of the predictive system, as well<br>as the data used to train the models. Future early-warning systems could also benefit from|
|2963|incorporating direct observations and forecasts of fuel conditions, using satellite data, land-|
|2964<br>2965|surface models, and vegetation monitoring to track fuel status in near real time and project<br>future risks. Such an approach would provide a more complete representation of the|
|2966<br>2967<br>2968|<br>processes governing wildfire activity, enabling earlier and more physically grounded warnings<br>of extreme fire risk and improving preparedness for the most consequential wildfire events.|
|2969<br>2970<br>|For the attribution analysis, the collaboration with World Weather Attribution (WWA) enables<br>a wider range of model attribution to be performed and lessons learned from the Rapid<br>|
|2971|Attribution Studies regularly performed by WWA helped strengthen community input into the|
|2972|<br>process. Diagnosing the fire weather conditions that play a key part in the attribution of|
|2973|extreme events was further improved with the addition of the FWI7X metric to give a more|
|2974|holistic view of fire weather, representing both short-term peaks and longer duration fire|
|2975|anomalies. Through the three methods presented in this report we are also able to report on|
|<br>2976|,<br>different aspects of the attribution question: emergence from natural variability through the|
|2977<br>2978<br>2979|<br>WWA approach (‘are events like this becoming more likely because of climate change?’);<br>attribution to external forcing through the CMIP6 large ensemble (‘how much does climate<br>change influence events of this magnitude?’); and event-specific attribution using HadGEM3-|
|<br>2980<br>2981<br>|<br>A (‘how much did climate change influence this specific event?’). Quantifying these different<br>measures of uncertainty results in different levels of confidence around the central estimate,<br>|
|2982<br>2983<br>2984<br>2985|but ultimately, the results all broadly agree; climate change has increased the probability of<br>an extreme event such as the ones witnessed in 2025-26, and this gives us an improved<br>understanding of how anthropogenic forcing is altering extremes.|
|2986<br>|Other areas of progression in this ongoing community effort include the expansion of multiple<br>|
|2987|independent fire models in the future projection analysis. In addition to ConFLAME projections,|
|2988|we incorporate FWI-based projections that isolate atmospheric drivers and include results|
|2989|from the BuRNN and Haas et al models which assess chanes in mean BA with fire size|
|<br>2990<br>|., ,   g      ,<br>for the first time, allowing us to assess other fire characteristics beyond BA. This builds on<br>|
|2991<br>|lessons from last year, in which reliance on a single model made interpreting region-specific<br>|
|2992|results more challenging, particularly when competing mechanisms yielded uncertain signals|
|2993|<br>(see Southern California in Kelley et al. 2025). Bringing together these complementary|
|2994<br>|methods developed across multiple research groups strengthens confidence in the overall<br>|
|2995<br>|direction of change.|
|2996||
|2997|However, our results this year also highlight both the strengths and remaining limitations of|
|2998|<br>current climate models. Across multiple results, the reanalysis, numerical models and GCMs|
|2999|generally agree on the direction of large-scale influences of climate change, consistently<br>|
|3000|indicating an increase in the likelihood and severity of extreme fire weather and BA This|
|<br>3001|.<br>agreement across independent models and methods provides greater confidence in the|
|3002|<br>robustness of the overall signal, especially for future projections where there is still no|
|3003|<br>alternative to using GCMs. However, differences do emerge primarily in the magnitude of the|
|3004|response. We see this in the FWI attribution for both Iberia and Chile, with the very different|
|3005<br>|scale of results between the models and reanalysis, and in the ConFLAME BA where the<br>|
|3006|future influence of climate change from modelled climate is much weaker than in the present-|
|3007<br>|<br>day climate constrained by observations. These differences  reflect  the continuing challenge<br>|
|3008|of capturing the most extreme events and their tail risks, rather than disagreement over the|



81 

https://doi.org/10.5194/essd-2026-587 Preprint. Discussion started: 3 August 2026 _⃝_ c Author(s) 2026. CC BY 4.0 License. 





|3009<br>|underlying direction of change, and suggests a more blended approach of data and models<br>|
|---|---|
|3010<br>3011|could help improve our understanding of the influence of climate change on extremes.|
|3012<br>3013<br>3014<br>3015<br>3016<br>3017<br>3018|Finally, a key next step is to strengthen the report’s role as a policy-relevant evidence base,<br>by more explicitly connecting its strands of analysis to impacts, and broadening the range of<br>contributing expertise. While the report has made strong progress as a community-driven<br>effort, key areas such as hydroclimatic driver analysis, attribution, and future projection<br>modelling remain dominated by contributions from Europe and parts of the Americas.<br>Expanding participation from other regions, and strengthening links with international<br>modelling efforts such as FireMIP, will not only improve the robustness of the science but also|
|3019<br>3020<br>3021<br>3022|ensure that the evidence base reflects a wider range of fire regimes and expertise. Together,<br>these advances will enhance the relevance, credibility, and usability of the report, providing a<br>stronger foundation for informing policy and decision-making.|
|<br>3023<br>3024|**7.5**<br>**Linking science to action**|
|3025<br>3026|This report's findings give wildfire policy a stronger scientific base. Its value, though, depends<br>on what happens next: how these findings move from research into decisions, training, tools|
|3027<br>3028|<br>and day-to-day fire management, linking to action.  Scientific advances have substantially<br>improved our ability to analyse weather, climate, fuels, and fire impacts, but reducing wildfire|
|3029<br>3030<br>3031<br>3032<br>|risk also depends on how people prepare for, respond to, and recover from fire events. In this<br>sense, a complementary report is being developed that will focus specifically on these societal<br>dimensions, extending the physical assessment presented here. Organisations such as<br>Global Fire Management Hub (FAO, 2026) hosted by FAO can also help fill this gap.<br>’|
|3033|Coordinated platforms for integrated fire management can help turn the reports evidence into|
|3034<br>3035|practical support for countries, regions and local teams, serving as a central platform for<br>practitioners. This could include capacity-building, technical guidance, policy advice and tools|
|3036<br>3037|that can be adapted to different ecosystems and fire-risk contexts, as well as space for peer-<br>to-peer exchange between practitioners, researchers and policymakers. This matters|
|3038<br>|<br>because, as we highlight in this report, there can be very different fire challenges across<br>|
|3039|regions. In some places, fires are becoming more severe in ecosystems that are not adapted|
|3040<br>3041<br>3042|to burn. In others, especially in Arctic and subarctic areas, wildfire risk is emerging in ways<br>that demand faster learning and adjustment.<br>|
|3043<br>3044|By linking the report’s findings with implementation work, scientific evidence can move closer<br>to the people designing and applying wildfire risk reduction strategies on the ground. That|
|3045<br>3046|<br>connection is essential if countries are to respond to changing fire regimes with approaches<br>that are both evidence-based and suited to local realities. Many projects are now applying|
|3047<br>3048<br>|current scientific findings to regional and national programs to help improve their ability to<br>adapt, mitigate and manage fires. Recent political commitments have also solidified the<br>|
|3049|rationale for an increased integrated fire management. The Kananaskis Wildfire Charter|
|<br>3050<br>3051|<br>endorsed by G7 nations in June 2025 (Prime Minister of Canada, 2025), the Leader’s Call to<br>Action on Integrated Fire Management and Wildfire Resilience at COP30 in Brazil (FAO,|
|3052<br>3053|2026), the 7th Session of the United Nations Environment Assembly (UNEA-7) on<br>strengthening the Global Management of Wildfires (PIB Delhi, 2025) and the two resolutions|
|3054<br>3055|regarding disaster risk reduction adopted by the UN General Assembly in 2024 and 2025<br>(United Nations, 2025), called for enhanced action on fire management. These political|
|3056<br>3057<br>3058<br>3059<br>3060<br>3061<br>|<br>commitments provided evidence that countries need better data, a greater emphasis on<br>innovation, stronger recognition of Indigenous fire stewardship and more investment in risk<br>reduction and preparedness, converting declarations into action. The findings of this report<br>support these commitments by providing crucial information needed for integrated fire<br>management.|
